Tenders.

WARRINGTON UNION. TENDERS.

_ Persons willing to contract for the supply of any of the undermentioned articles, to be delivered free of expense at the Workhouse, Warrington, or. Cottage Homes, Padgate, at such times, and in such quantities, as the Guardians or their officers may require, are requested to send in Tenders to me on or before 10 a.m., Wednesday, the 22nd September, 1915, and the same will be considered by the Guardians at a meeting to be held on the same date. Tenders are required : From Ist October, 1915, to 31st September, 1916—Sweeping chimneys. From Ist October, 1915, to 31st March, 1916 :-- Coal and Slack. Butchers' Meat. Clogs. Flour and Oatmeal. *Crockeryware. Drapery. Boots, Shoes & Slippers. Wines and Spirits. ' Coffins. Drugs. Brooms and Brushes. *Surgical Dressings. Leather & Shoemakers' Soda Water.

Necessaries. Paints, &c. Tailors' Goods. *lronmongeu. Bacon, Butter, Cheese, Thirds. Lard, Margarine, &c. Beer and Stout. Tobacco and Snuff. *Electrical Goods. Fish and Ice. *Kitchen Utensils. Groceries & Necessaries. Skim Milk.

--*No goods of German or Austrian origin will be accepted. Separate tenders must be sent for the Work- house and Cottage Homes, and samples of the articles requited can be seen at either institu- tions between the hours of 10 to 12 noon and 2 to 4 p.m. The fuel to be supplied must be according to the specifications as set out on, the tender form, and quotations must be made for delivery, either to the siding adjoining the Workhouse, or by cart, and by cart to the Cot- tage Homes. Forms of tender, and instructions to Persons tendering (which mkt be strictly complied with), may be obtained from the undersigned. All accounts will be: paid. monthly. The Guardians do not necessarily bind them- selves to accept the lowest or any tender. By order, . ARTHUR BOTTOMLEY. Clerk to the Guardians.

Bewsey Chambers. September 3rd, 1915

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Those Teeth.

Mr. Arthur Mounfield's story of the village rector's teeth reminded me of an incident at & mayoral Sunday service at which I was present. The borough in question was then newly incorporated, and on the occasion re- ferred to there was a large and representative ' congregation at the parish church. The senior curate, who read the prayers, had just detained a full set of artificial teeth. He articulated with difficulty, and from the out- set it was obvious that he was very uncom- fortable. Presently his voice completely changed; although he spoke more freely, he had now an extraordinary lisp. At the same time I noticed unusual levity on the part of members of the choir. I afterwards asked one of them what they were laughing at, and he told me that the curate had convulsed them by popping his offending " peggies " into his handkerchief. Embarrassing MS.

In his amusing chapter of stories, Mr. Mounfield repeats that of the clergyman whose manuscript gave him trouble owing to the failing light, and who sent the clerk with it to the window to read an obscure sentence for him. I was once in a village church when the pulpit was occupied by an eccentric vicar of a nqighbouring parish. In reading his ser- mon he floundered a good deal, and eventu- ally, suddenly throwing up his arms to em- phasise a point, he caught the loose leaves with the tips of his fingers, and sent them flying over the heads of the occupants of the front pews! Amid the ill-concealed merri- ment some at least gave a thought to the un- fortunate preacher, but he did not seem in the least perturbed, though he was obliged to conclude somewhat abruptly. Respecting Smoking.

As I rea,d on the front page of this paper (vide advertiiied appeal for tobacco fund) that sixpence will make a fighting member-of our South Lancashires happy for a week, I thought how moderate this is as contrasted with some other countries and some former periods in our own. Although tobacco was not smoked regularly in these islands until after Sir Walter Raleigh introduced it at Myrtle Grove, -Y oughal, so long ago as 1723 there was a competition at Oxford in which a prize was to be given to the one who could smoke out three ounces of tobacco first with- out drinking or going off the stage, and this was actually won. Moreover, I have some- where read that at a club in Derbyshire a smoker consumed a pound of shag tobacco at one sitting. A teapot was the pipe, and the man smoked through the spout. A modern cigarette smoker wrote a while ago: "I commenced in 1868, and have con- tinued the habit to the present day! . I smoke when I wake at night, I smoke be- tween courses at dinner, and I smoke all day, but I have yet to discover that it has done me any harm after forty years of the habit." I have known at least two doctors who smoked cigarettes in every spare moment. Such excess is to be deprecated, but, while it is impossible to commend these examples, one cannot but sympathise with the moderate smokers in the trenches. They must on no account be denied their reasonable indulgence nor have to beg in vain for "fags."

Cricketing Ministers. In his " Memories " Dean Hole discussec the question " Should parish clergymen play cricket?" What I read therein brought to my mind the story of the cricketing parson who one Sunday, after reading the lessons,. made the startling announcement, "Here endeth the first innings." I knew of a Wesleyan minister who was a first-class bowler, and once in a match one of an opposing team who had a straight ball into his wicket from the cleric said, "If that parson can preach as well as he bowls, he should be worth hearing." He went to hear him, and became a good .sup- porter of the minister in his. work. ' Rubbing It In.

The reader may thank or blame the youth- ful winner of last •week's "Favourite Games" competition for these paragraphs, his avowed preference for cricket having set my thoughts in. this direction. The organist at Marlbor- ough School, who used to select hymns for the evening 'services at the school' chapel when members of visiting teams usually stayed for service, tells a story of W. G. Grace. The famous cricketer had visited the school, and was bowled first ball by a schoolboy, and one of the hymns sung contained the line: "The scanty triumphs grace has won."

===== 0002601_19150904_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
The Work Shirkers.

The case of Alexander Edwards, who was last week committed for three months for attempted imposition, leads me to note that a favourite device at present is to pretend to be an old acquaintance invalided home from the Front. " You don't remember me, do you? Ah, well, I can't blame you, for I must have altered a lot after what I've gone through, But I used to meet you "—then follows the ingenious yarn, ending with the inevitable appeal. The wiles of the beggar constantly vary with circumstances, and the tricks and dodges by which professional men- dicants practise on the unwary would supply material ,for many columns.

Curicus Bets. The local Police Court case arising out of a het on a trifling matter brought to my mind what I once read .in Mr. Ralph Neville's "Light come, light go," regarding an eccen- tric wager made in 1740 by Lord - George Orford. The latter bet'another .nobleman a large sum that a drove of geese would beat an equal number of turkeys in a race from Norwich to London. Many years ago I was told of Chinese gamblers who would sit for hours with sugar before them, each betting .** that the next fly to alight would come upon his particular lump. I very well remember the prosecution of the Park Club in 1884. It . practically brought to an end .fashionable gambling clubs; such private ventures of the kind as we have nowadays are not allowed a long run. Gambling was at its wildest in this c country in the latter part of the eighteenth -century—that age of extraordinary folly, in which men played half their lives and for all their possessions. The Regent himself gambled regularly, and Charles James Fox, the statesman, was conspicuous by his reck- lessness in this respect. It would be easy to enlarge here, but it may suffice to assure any inclined to gloomy views of the gambling evil in our midst that things have been very much worse in this respect.

"Music and Healing."

This headlipe in last week's paper somehow reminded me of the formation not far short of a quarter of a century ago of a Guild of St.. Cecilia, with the moving spirits of which an acquaintance of mine had some correspond- ence. The objects were to be:, (1) To test, by trials made in a large number. of cases of illness, the power of soft music to induce calni- ness-of mind, alleviation of pain, and -sleep. (2) To provide -a large 'number of Specially trained musicians who shall be in, readiness to answer promptly the summons of a physi- ician. (3) To provide a large hall in a central part of London, in which music shall be given throughout' all hours- of the day and night. This music to be conveyed by telephone .at- tached to certain wards in each of the chief London hospitals. (4) Obtaining records and collecting funds. Trials were made and the general effect was mental tranquility, sleep, -diminution of pain in many instances, and fall of temperature. The musicwas a solace t.o those in great pain and never irritating so long as it was soft. That headline, " Music and heal; jug," would appear to have indicated some- thing far above and beyond what was imine- Aliately denoted.

VOLUNTARYISM.

" The Veteran " Discourses Learnedly On Its Advantages.

OUR WONDERFUL ARMY.

It was a knock! And when I opened the door there stood my friend the Veteran. He carried in his hand a newspaper, and his countenance bore the signs of a disturbed temper. "Were you afraid I would be out?" I asked, as he entered. " Out ! No, my lad. Sorry I knocked so loud, hut—well, I feel vexed," he answered sharply. Generally of a quiet disposition, I wondered what had upset the old man; and when he was at last comfortably seated, he handed Ins his newspaper, with a request to read a certain blue-lead marked leading article. I was not long in discovering what had disturbed him, for the leader writer had been severely expres- sing views in favctur of Conscription. "Well, what do you think of it?" he in- quired, as I handed him back the paper. "Rather rough on our Voluntary system," I replied. "Rough," he ejaculated. "I should think so. Fancy the likes of such people upsetting the country at such a critical period. It is a shame. By Jove, if. I were Prime Minister—. But there, one cannot help getting excited over such a mad act." I should imagine it is rather a foolish policy to introduce such a subject at present?" I queried. "Foolish, I wish I could find a better name," he said determinedly. "To think that our present Voluntary system has been • such a success, even neutral countries commenting favourably upoh it,'and then for men to try to divide the country with such a hare-brained scheme. Yes, Lord Hugh Cecil is right when he says that it will cause a "crop of ill-feeling and party bitterness.'" • The old man was Indeed wild, and I asked him whether he thought' the present state of affairs did not justify a change.. MILITARISM, PRUSSIAN AND 'BRITISH. "Certainly not!" he replied quickly. "Our Voluntary system has always succeeded, despite attempts of a similar character. But lam surprised at you thinking about a change. Change! Why, my lad, • our victory • over Prussian militarism depends upon the way in which we maintain our freedom in matters of this sort. Only the other day I read a letter from a soldier at the Front in which he said, 'I sinceiely hope one thing, and that is that the Conscriptionist party do not get the upper hand in England, for if they do we might as well be under. German rule, for the liberty on which we have set such store and for which we are now fighting will be a thing of the past.' That's straight from the shoulder for you, eh?" "Well, yes; but what objects have the Con- scriptionists in coming forward with their policy at the present time, do you think F" He smiled. "That is a question which has puzzled a large number of people. Some say we were unprepared, and we must avoid it in future. Very good! But to tell you the truth, lad, I believe they cannot answer your query straightforwardly. They do not know how many men we have, nor how many we are likely to get. And I don't blame Lord Kitchener for not letting them know." . Asked whether Lord Kitchener had mentioned anything about Conscription,, the old man said, "Never a word, so far as I have read. But he would not, for fear of being accused of taking sides, as you call it. But I do know this that when he spoke quite rekently, at the Guild- hall, I believe, he said he was perfectly satis- fied as to the present results of our Voluntary system. In fact, although he was at the time appealing for more Men, he said that 'The pre- sent new armies had already reached a figure which only a short while ago woulu have been considered utterly unth4dcable.' I call that a complete vindication of our present system." "but

"Just so," I said, as I interrupted him, why did he appeal for more men?" "Trying to draw me, eh?" he chuckled "Well that is easily answered. He forecasts a long struggle, and maybe heavy casualties, and his object in appealing for more men is in order to keep up the present strength of the Army—a drain on whieh. in view of a long war, will be apparent to all,"

===== 0002601_19150904_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
FIT AND UNMARRIED;

"But the Conscriptionists say that there are a vast number of physically fit unmarried men who have not joined the Army," I suggested. "How do they know?" he thundered. "Have they deducted how many are wanted for the manufacture of munitions, the number re- quired in agricultural pursuits? Aye, and more than that, what about other important industries which have to be kept going?" "Yes, there is a great deal in that," I said, " but- "

"No buts in it, my boy. Trust an old man for knowing. It is, to my mind, a dog in the manger policy. A good case of 'lf we can't rule the roost, we will be angry, and won't play properly.' And a pretty nice mess they would make of things if they- had their own way. I remember how the other week Mr. W. Long tried to clip their wings, if only for the sake of peace. He was speaking on the ques- tion as to whether the Government would make a change in the military policy, and said that 'Everybody who loves his country and has studied her history must hope with all his heart that we shall be able to maintain .what we call the Voluntary system. It has given us a wonderful Army, which has covered itself with glory.' Why, lad, the Conscriptionists' cause is hopeless, yes, hopeless," and the old 'man chuckled at the thought of it. "So you are fully satisfied with our present system, and from your long years of experience I take it for granted you do not think Con- scription will ever be needed?" I asked, as he rose to go.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
A GRAND THOUGHT.

At this query he sat down again, and put- ting his hand into the inner pocket of his coat drew out a bundle of slips of paper. Selecting two from among the lot he said firmly, -.l' Con- scription will • never be needed. 'Just listen to this. It is a cutting from a speech made by the Lord Mayor of London to the Volunteers when they were being sworn in at the Guild- hall, London, at the commencement of the South African War. I kept it by me in order that it would rouse me up if ever I thought that our Voluntary system was dying out. He said, 'You are here with the deliberate inten- tion of voluntarily taking upon yourselves the hardships and dangers incidental to a serious campaign. The object you have in view is worthy of your great sacrifice. The determination which impels your conduct animates the manhood of the nation.' And what of this? I culled it from the paper the other day. A miner wrote to say he had some of his boys in the Army, and, he continued, What did I encourage my boys to go for? Why, to help to put down and defeat Prussian militarism; and do Conscriptionists think that we shall submit tamely to have the same sys- tem fastened on us? We love our British insti- tutions, with all their faults; and it is for these and our freedom that we have so readily responded."Fhere, my lad, that is the spirit which is going to keep out Conscription from our midst."

"I should imagine that the facts contained in letters and statements like those you have quoted, ought to have an impression on the Con- scriptionists?". I Ventured to suggest, as he finally got up to depart. , "Well, they ought to do," he replied. "But whether they, do or not; it is a grand thought to me as an old..man and a firm; believer in the Voluntary system, to be able to glory in the fact that one of the proudest traits in the manhood of our nation. is the adherence to the traditions of their fareNthers in rallying to the flag, with out compulsion, to defend their country in its hour of need. Good night, my lad." R.J.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 4, 1915. FARM AND GARDEN. WASTE OF EGGS.

CROPS FROM "BARREN " LAND.

Al Fine Object-Lesson.

An important experiment in agriculture, is being conducted in Somerset. The Mendip 11;11s have a well-deserved reputation for a Wild and picturesque beauty (writes a "Daily Chronicle" correspondent), but hitherto they have been largely unproductive, and, it was thought, un- profitable. Recently, a farmer 'famed Ball acquired and ploughed up a large plot of so- called " barren " land in the region of Black- down—over 800 ft. above sea-level, and the high- est part of the Mendips. He was able to rent the land at a peppercorn rent, and proceeded forthwith to clear it of heather, gorse, rocks, &c., and he has now succeeded in raising good crops of oats, and of. turnips and other roots.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
RECORD WHEAT CROPS.

We wonder how many of our agricultural readers have ever thought of being able to grow 10 quarters and 101 quarters of wheat per acre? Many will perhaps regard this crop as out of the question considering that the average yield for Great Britain is only about 4 quarters per acre, and that really bumper crops rarely come above 7or 71 quarters per acre. Yet such crops as 10 and 101 quarters per acre have oeen grown in this country,: and not in isolated cases, but in several districts, including Cornwaii, Kent, Sussex and the Lothians of Scotland. The breed of wheat which has produced such record yields as this is the Victor variety, a new breed introduced by Messrs. Gartons, Limited, the well-known agriculturhl plant breeders, of Wtq- rington, some few years ago. In their catalogue of wheats and other seeds for autumn sowing just published, Messrs. Gartons give full par- ticulars Of this . valuable wheat, together with other new and regenerated breeds, including Benefactor, a new wheat introduced las. year for the first time. How it has been possible to breed such heavy-yielding wheats is fill*, ex- plained in this catalogue, and No. invite all interested agriculturists who have not already received one to write direct to Messrs. Gartons' head office at Warrington for a copy, which will be gladly forwarded post, free.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
FORMS OF STACKS.

The shape in which corn stacks are built is largely a matter of local custom, but there is something to be said for and against the differ- ent forms. There are several advantages about an oblong stack with rounded ends to the roof. It is easier to build than a gable end, because there are no shah) corners to keep up, and these are the most difficult part in building. The rounded ends are also handier to thatch than the flat portion of the ordinary hipped roof. But the easiest of all to thatch, and the most economical in thatching material, is the obbang stack with gable ends. The appearance of such a stack is very smart, but it requires skilful building to keep the ends straight and the roof of .correct form. The chief disadvan- tage is that there is a large propertioy. of corn under the roof, and this part is seldom in such good condition as that in the body of the stack. There remains for consideration the round stack. This also has .a smart appearance, and many find it the easiest to build. .It holds a great deal of corn for the size of its circumfer- ence, and there is not much waste under the roof, but it calls for the greatest skill in thatch- ing. Whatever form of stack is selected, the walls should be made to bulge out gently from the foundation to the eaves, as this prevents rain from penetrating.—E.M.B. in "Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19150904_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
SUCCESS OF A HARVEST CAMP.

Interesting testimony to the value of the ser- vices of 'volunteer workers in the harvest field has reached "The Times" from Norfolk. Mr. Henry Overman, Kipton House, Weasenham, Swaffham, writes: "Labour is short, although lam paying ,t 9 each man'for .the harvest. lam running a harvest camp of about twenty men— all schoolmasters. The 'schools represented are Eton, .TJppingham, Royal Naval College, Dart- mouth, Berkhampsted, Aldenham, St. Edward's School, Oxford. Woodbridge, Trent College, Derby, Mill Hill, and several others. The men are a great success, working hard and doing their work particularly well. Ho* they have caught on to the work. AI extraordinary ; no day seems too long for them. They will shorten my harvest by quite a week, .which is good for both my men and myself. If L could have these men all the year round, what a comfort it would be! In two months they could be trained as 'perfect agricultural labourers,' who could do any skilled work."

===== 0002601_19150904_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

NEW ROSES.

Comparatively few of the new roses—and there are hundreds in the course of a few years—are ever grown in gardens. The flowers may be of a distinct and pretty shade of colour, but if the plants lack vigour and freedom of flowering they are of very little value! Raisers too often think more of the individual flower than of the habit of the plant, and the worst possible place to make a selection is the exhibition tent. From seeing a few blooms on the exhibition board one gets no idea of. their value for the garden,

===== 0002601_19150904_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
WINTER SPINACH.

Many people have an idea that unless winter spinach is sown in August the growth will not be sufficiently advanced to be of any value dur- ing the winter months. One often sees ground from which a crop of onions has been h'iarvested remain bare during the winter. If, however, the autumn is favourable, as the present one is, spinach may well occupy the ground on which the onions have been grown, more especially if the soil is on the light side. The spinach niay be sown as late as the middle of September, and if all goes well it will form a good succession to the August-sown batch. As the soil from which the, 'onions- have. been .cleared has been. well manured, all that is necessary is to draw the lines and sow the seed;

===== 0002601_19150904_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
HOLLYHOCKS.

It is disappointing to find so few of these noble flowers in gardens. .Very often a finer display is seen in the cottager's garden than in a large garden. The disease which at one time used to be so troublesome has not been so rife of late years, and this, no doubt, is due to sowing every year seeds of a good strain, :the old plants as soon as they show any liability to disease being rooted out and burnt. This has helped to baffle the malady, owing to the young plants having so much more vitality. It is not yet too late to sow seed in pans or boxes, which Should be placed in a frame or greenhouse. Then later, when the seedlings are strong enough, they can be pricked out into pans or potted singly, and wintered in a frame, to be planted out next April. If given well-manured, deeply-trenched soil many of these seedlings will flower well in the autumn, and in the following season even more freely.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
WHITE DROOPING CAMPANULA.

Where a plant of .trailing or drooping habit is required, there is nothing, to equal this (C. iso- phylla alba). Commencing to flower early in the summer, it will keep- up a. succession of bloom for many weeks. Few things are so 'easily managed if ,given .at the Start a good larder and if, attention is paid to the watering, with an occasional dose of artificial manure. Suspended in the cottage window as a pot plant it is excel- lent, and the same may be said of it for window- boxes. Ciriotisly enough, it thrives in either or iliasde, though in the latter position the flowers remain in good condition longer. This question of position is, however, of little moment, as the flower buds develop so rapidly that the plants keep up a continuous show of bloom for a long time. Lhave,two window-boxes faced with this, the plants 'in which. have been flowering freely for the past four weeks, and are still full of bloom. It is strange that a plant of such easy culture, and quite hardy, should only be seen here and there. A word. of warn- ing in the cultivation of.this plant is necessary. Do not, on the score of tidiness. when the beauty of the flowers has gone, cut them off, as this will affect the display in the following 'year. Let the flowers remain until the stalks as well as the-flowers have quite dried up, and then no harm will come from trimming them off.—Wick in "Farm and illoma."

===== 0002601_19150904_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
Rolled Out And Washed Down

A Drain!

At Tower Bridge Police Court, the licensee of the Grapes public-house Bermondsey Wall, complained of the stench from the destruction by the Bermondsey "Borough Council of huge quantities of bad eggs at a wharf opposite his premises. Mr. Clarke, who represented the Council, said that owing to the war there was an abnormal number of bad eggs on the market. Tooley-street was the egg market for Great Britain, and it was the duty of the Borough Council to remove all the bad eggs as trade refuse or unsound food. The complaint was not groundless, but they were taking all pos- sible means to destroy the eggs in the least objectionable way. The complainant said the state of things was worse than ever that morn- ing, and he was told they, had now another 4,000 cases of eggs to destroy. "They run a roller .over the eggs," he said, "and then wash them down a drain. Think of 4,000 cases of bad eggs being smashed outside your house !" Mr. Clarke explained that the eggs were crushed and drained into the sewer, which yvds afterwards flushed• with water and :h stronK disinfectant; and Dr, Brown, the meciteal officer of health, said that wherever they destroyed them there would be complaints, so that they were between the devil and the- ddep sea. They would niake further experiments ..t& see if a less objectionable method could be employed.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE WHEAT AND OATS.

Barley Acreage The Lowest On Record.

_ The preliminary statement of the agricul- tural returns for England and Wales, collected in June last,.shows a decrease in the total area under crops and grass of 61,000 acres, of which 33,000 acres represents arable land and 28,000 acres permanent grass. Wheat shows an in- _ crease of 363,000 acres, or 20 per cent., as coin- pared with—laSt 'year, the 2,170,000 acres under this cereal being the largest recorded since 1891; -while the increase since 1913 amounts to 469,000 acres, or nearly 28 per cdnt. Oats have increased by 158,000 acres. These increases of the two most important corn crops have been obtained mainly by substituting them for barley, beans, peas and roots. Barley shows a decrease of 273,000 acres, bringing the total under this crop to the lowest on record. Turnips and swedes have fallen off by 113,000 to 932,000' acres, this being the first occasion on which the total has fallen below a million acres. Hay (whether " seeds " or meadow) has decreased by 147,000 acres. The decrease in hay. may largely be attributed to the dry spring, as the area of permanent grass reserved for grazing has increased by 102,000 acres, while clovers and seeds for grazing are about the same as in 1914. The other most important crop for human consumption—potatoes—shows an increase of abeut 1,800 acres. All other crops. except cabbage and Kohlrabi, show decreases of-greater or snialler extent.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
ENCOURAGING LIVE STOCK FIGURES.

The live stock. returns show increases among cattle and sheep, but decreases among horses and swine. Homes, many of which have been taken for the Army, have fallen in number by 112,000 (or 8 per cent.), the greatest relative decrease being among horses not used for agri- cultural purposes. Cows show a decrease of 50,000 from the rec'ord ftgure of 1914, but are still higher than in any other year; all other cattle have increased, and the total number 6,064,000 (186,000 more than in 1914), constitutes the highest recorded. The increase in sheep amounts to 263,000; those above one year (other than breeding ewes) increasing by a third of a million; but an indifferent lambing season is responsible for a reduction of 101,000 in the number of lambs. The decline of 61,000 in pigs is mostly among breeding sows, but the total is well ,above the ten-year average.

FIRES AT BURTONWOOD.

ncendiaries Suspected To Have Been At Work.

About midnight on Friday last week, a fire broke out in a Dutch barn belonging to Mr. Herbert Houghton, Burtonwood. The War- rington Fire ,Brigade was called up and, arriving with their usual: promptness, found that two stacks of new hay and half a stack of old were in flames. It was recognised at the outset that the task of averting the destruction of the hay was virtually hoyeless, but the brigade rendered yeoman service in preventing the destruction of the roof of the barn and damage to the adjacent buildings. It is calculated that the damage amounts to about £6OO, but it is covered by insurance. , Another fire occurred about the same time on a farm near by. and both are attributed to incendiaries. The other fire was noticed by some caliers before it had obtained a formid- able hold, and pails of 'water 'met the situation. It is stated that two men were seen, about the time of the fire,, running across the 'fields adjoining the farm.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
THIRTY-FOUR YEARS' OLD WHEAI

Thirty-four- years ago Mr. Philip Selby, of Aisby, South Lin,colnshire, harvested a field of wheat, and when the harvest was over he de- clared he would not thresh it until it attained a cettain price. What that price was ;no one ever knew but himself, not even his nearest rela- tive, and about a year ago Mr. Selby died, taking .his secret with-him. The stack contained the produce of about 9 acres. •On half a dozen oeca- Mons, at least, it has been thatched. Often was the owner twitted by his fellow farmers regard. ing the stack, but Mr. Selby was a man who always faithfully kept his word, was obdurate, and to this day, had he lived, that stack would probably now ,be standing. Succeeded in the business by his son, however, the latter decided that the old stack should be threshed, and, • although thirty-four years-- have elapsed since it was harvested, the grain was found to be in excellent condition. Mr. Henry Bell, of Gran- tham, has just bought the wheat for 60s. per quarter, and the delivery. has proved to be about three quarters to the acre, the corn being exceedingly fine, weighing about 60i1b. to the bushel, and Mr. Bell declares it is better than any he has milled for thirty years: The pur- chaser has had some of the wheat made into bread, and very good it is. It is questionable whethernny parallel can be found in the annals of British agriculture, and it is hoped that a sample of the wheat may be preserved at some agricultural college or museum for the edifica- tion, mit only of : the present, but of future, generations.—" Field."

BIRDS OF PREY.

Sidelights On The" Fun Of The Fair" At Warrington.

THREE " HUMORISTS"

FINED.

"'All the fun of the fair! Threepence when you knock three down!" This style of war-cry must frequently have been heard by devotees of fairground frivolities. Often, however, the "fun" is too one-sided to be really humorous, and illustrations of the point were forthcoming at Tuesday morning's sitting of the Warrington Borough Bench, Councillor Henry Roberts pre- siding. LavilLia Wheatley, living in a van on the Warrington Fairground, was summoned for obtaining by false pretences the "sum- of four- pence from Albert Henry Buddle, on the 14th of August. Lawrence Taylor, of 20, Heath- side, was summoned for a similar offence in respect of threepence; and George Jennings, resident in a Nian in the Market-place, appeared to: answer an accusation in regard to a like sunk _ _ _ _

For the sake of saving time the defendants were asked if they were willing for the cases to be heard together, but one of the three objected to such s a proceeding, and accordingly each offender came separately into the limelight of the law.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
Skittles That Were Not Skittles.

The Chief Constable, treating with the first defendant—Lavinia Wheatley—said that the young man Buddle paid for three balls and shied them at what were apparently skittles. Any- one Who knocked three down had threepence. That was gaming, in any case. The skittles were intended to be wooden. That was accord- ing to the. definition in any dictionary. About half the apparent skittles were genuine, but the other half were metal dummies weighing something like fourteen pounds each. It was practically a physical impossibility to knock these -down with wooden balls weighing two ounces, which was the approximate weight of the balls used. The skittles and the heavy dummies were so arranged that it was impos- siblefor anyone to knock three down with one ball. One or two on the top were wooden ones, and those underneath were of iron. Detective- Sergeant Reddington and Sergeant Metcalfe were with Buddle when he attempted to knock three skittles over, and they took the matter ii hand.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
Notice Disowned.

DeteUive-Sergeant Reddington said that he saw sever4l other young men, besides Buddle, try to knock the skittles over, but none of them could manage to capsize anything in the bottom row, although they frequently hit them. The balls would rebound almost into the faces of the onlookers. ile watched the proceedings at several other stalls, and when he came back to the first stall he found the skittles (genuine and. dummy) were all mixed up together in a sack. Most of them were of iron. Witness said to defendant: "Are they wooden?" andg.te- ceived the reply: "No; they're mostly iron." Asked what she meant by deceiving the public in that way, defendant said: "Oh, it's done all over the country, and nothing is ever said." On the canvas of the .stall was written in large -letters "All these skittles are wood." When defendant's attention was called to this notice she said it was not her notice, but that it belonged to the next stall.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
Only A Foundation.

Defendant said that it was false to say that there were only two skittles on top. There were three. It was not expected of customers that they would knock the bottom ones over. The bottom ones were only used to put the wooden ones on. Albert Henry Buddk, a. clerk in the police office, said that he hit the skittles several times, but could not knock over anything in the bottom row.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
A Building Exhibition.

Defendant said that false -statements had been made as to the number of wooden skittles on top, and, at her request, she was allowed to treat the Court to a building exhibition, with the edge of the defendant's box as a foundation. The Bench, however, were not satisfied, and fined her forty shillings and costs, offering her the option of a month's imprisonment.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
CASE THE SECOND.

The second defendant was Lawrence Taylor, against whom it was stated that all his skittles were of iron.

Defendant said he had been at the business for years and had never had a complaint be- fore.

The Chief Constable: If he has been doing this for so many years, he -must be very, very well off, and it's painful to me that I can't prefer further charges against him. It's high- way robbery. An Oracle Speaks.

John Edwards, of 18, Norman-street, War- rington, said that it was a regular practice with him to go into the Fair on Saturday night and make what he could.

Asked by the Chief what he made on the average, witness said that he made some- times from three to four shillings on the outlay of one shilling. The Chief: Oh, I see. Sergeant Reddington, take his name and address for gaming. The witness laughed, but the Chief, with one of his eagle* glances, quelled this outburst of levity, and demanded to know: "Do you think it's fair to take this man's money like this?"

" Well," witness replied, "he's got a fair chance of getting mine." Questioned on the point of whether he knew the skittles were not all of wood, witness • said he knew the bottom ones *ere not wooden, be- cause he had shied at them so long. He had knocked three iron " skittles " down with three balls.

The Chief.: Doesn't he bar you? Witness: No. I go every Saturday. I could knock them down now, if I had the balls. The Chief: You are a sort of decoy, I sup- pose, for the ordinary customers? The. witness p.olite)y, Kppudiated, the allega, tion with a laugh and a shrug, whereat the Chief reiterated the remark with greater, assur- Ance to the magistrates. There was no doubt, he, said, that witness was a decoy bird.

A fiine of'4os., with costs, was imposed.

CASE THE THIRD.

The. casq against Geo. Jennings wa,s proved by Sergeant Melcal, who stated that, after watching the, stall, he haddt seen anyone knock three skittles down and accordingly spoke to the defendant, 'remarking that he did not think the skittles were genuine. De- fendant at 'once replied: "I am very sorry you have caught me. I know I ought to have had a notice up. I have a notice in the van. I will fetch it." He did so, and explained that some of the skittles were of wood and some of ivory. One of the skittles, the witness affirmed, weighed fifteen pounds. Defendant said that he himself could knock the heavy skittles down. Sergeant Metcalfe confirmed this statement. What The Solicitor Did.

The Chief said that, when questioned, de- fendant said ,that he had been fined forty shillings at Rochdale for a similar offence. Defendant said the fine itself did not amount to :forty shillings, adding, amidst laughter, that. the solicitor made it up to :.forty He denied that the game was robbery, 4,nd offered. to knock ,the.. skittles: over in Court. ' • • „t.The magistrates, however, had seen and heard enough of skittles for the day, and fined defendant £2 10s.

LOST RACK TO SAVE LIFE.,

Exciting, Episode During Sports At - Felixstowe.

During regimental sports at Felixstowe on Saturday,. Private Platten, of the Suffolk, was leading;handsomely in the final, for the quarter- mile swimming prize when he saw 'Sergeant 13ennett had been seized with cramp and was drowning. Platten abandoned the race and swam to his sinking comrade, whom he held until a boat arrived.

He was enthusiastically applauded by the soldiers on the pier and beach. Platten, of course, lost the race, but the officers awarded him a special prize and warmly thanked him.

IN THE COURTS.

ATTEMPTED ILLEGAL PAWNING.

Stiff Fine For A Warrington Woman.

Lily Gifford, of Ship-yard, was charged at the Borough Court on Tuesday with acting with a soldier, Robert Cain, in attempting to pawn military clothing, to wit a pair of Army boots, and also with giving a false name and address, contrary to the Aliens' Registration Act. Detective-Sergeant Reddington said that he was on duty in Bridge-street on Monday, 23rd ult., when he saw • Cain in the street saccom- panied by a woman carrying a bundle, which he found to contain a pair of military boots and a pair of underpants. The prisoner, it was ascertained, had taken the articles to Houghton's, the pawnbrokers, but the property had been rejected and the police communicated with.

A fine of 40s. was imposed in respect of the first charge, but on the second charge the accused was acquitted.

AT THE RATEPAYERS' EXPENSE.

Imprisonment For Desertion Of Wife

And Family.

At the Warrington Borough Court on Tues- day, before,. Councillor ,Henry,Robeits and Mr. C. Jackson Holmes, Peter McGuinness, of no fixed abode, was charged with running away from the Parish of Haydock, on February 9th, and leaving his wife and family chargeable to the Warrington Union.

It was stated that the prisoner had, by run- ning away, involved the Union in an expendi- ture of £6O.

A number of previous convictions against prisoner were proved. _ _ _

- Prisoner said he had tried to enlist and had not passed, but he intended to have another try when it was decided to form a fresh battalion. He was engaged at present in munition work.

A sentence of one month's imprisonment was imposed.

NOT PROVEN.

Allegations Of Habitual Drunkenness Fall Through.

Jas. Bostock, of 46, Bewsey-road, Warrington, appeared to an adjourned summons at the Borough Court on Tuesday, in respect of accusations by his wife that he was an habitual drunkard, grounds on which she desired a separation. The Chief Constable said that every possible inquiry had been made by the police, and no foundation could be discovered for the allega- tions made by defendant's wife. Superin- tendent Williamson had known the defendant for a long time and personally entirely dis- believed the wife's statements in the matter of her husband's indulgence in alcoholics. The testimony a men with whom the defendant worked was to the same effect.

The Chairman (Councillor Hy. Miberts) suggested to the wife that the best thing she could do was to withdraw the summons.

On the intimation by the / defendant's wife

that she was'prepared to fall in with the advice of the Bench, the Chairman, addressing de- fendant, asked: "If your• wife withdraws this, are you prepared to do all you can to make her comfdrtable and happy?" " Yes," said the defendant.

"Very well," the Chairman concluded, "this is another chance for you both to see if you can't get on better together."

WITHOUT A LIGHT.

Taxi Driver Blames The State Of The

Road.

Alfred Overston, of 10, Tanver's-lane, War- rington, was summoned at the County Police Court, Warrington, on Wednesday, for driving a motor car without a light after dark, on Sunday, the 22nd of August. It was stated that at 14.55 on the evening in question the defendant drove a taxi over Sankey Bridge without a light, and did not stop when the policeman blew his whistle. A quarter of an hour later he returned, and then the policeman stopped him and asked him if lie had not heard the whistle. He said "No." The lamp was still out. The defendant said he thought some consid- eration should be' shown on account of the state of the road. The lamp must have gone out going over Sankey Bridge.

A -fine- of I.os. was imposed, but defendant's licence was not endorsed. ,

===== 0002601_19150904_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
A BABE IN A WOOD.

Six Months For Woman Who Wanted Looking After.

Sarah Agnes Broderick, of no settled abode, was charged at the County Sessions, Warring- ton, 011 Wednesday, with neglecting her two- year-old child in such a manner as to cause it unnecessary suffering and injury to health. Mr. J. W. Knowles, who prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., said that the prisoner had been wandering about. She was a single woman. She had been at Wigan and later at Sea- forth, where she gave another child to a woman to hold and then cleared off. Defendant had been hanging about in the neighbourhood of Winwick for several months, and his (Mr. Knowles') gardener had seen her several times with soldiers and, on one occasion between four and five in the morning, asleep in a wet wood with the baby in her arms. He (Mr. Knowles) thought it was a case in which, for the woman's own sake (for she was more to be pitied than any- thing else); the extreme penalty of the law, so far as length of sentence went, should be in- flicted, but without hard labour. The woman needed looking after, and he thought a longish term of imprisonment would be a good thing for her.

• The defendant ,denied that she had been seen with more than one soldier, whose name —and whereabouts she mentioned.

A sentence of six months' imprisonment was imposed.

LEATHER.

Heavy Work And Quick Dispatches From Local Works.

Following the tendency of buyers to hold aloof, there has been something of a reaction, and business was fairly free again last week. The fact that the market is not at present an open one, and that as regaptis the classes most in use there is no isurplus of production, has apparently been impresked on the mind of the consumer, hence sales were brisk in the lines indicated., In goods not generally usable for Government work business was somewhat slack. and there has been more concentrated effort te depress, prices, but while it is reported that easier offers have been made by tanners, I be- lieve that this " information " comes par- ticularly from the London district, as I cannot *bear anything locally to substantiate Speaking for this district prices are firm, with advances unlikely, or desired; on anything but bellies; regarding the latter, I anticipate a con- siderable firming up in the autumn, although this •is contrary to the general opinion. The week's work has been heavy, and leather has gone out as quickly as ready. Apart from the customary heavy demand • for stout bends, there was a call equal in strength, if not in volurne, for bends from 101 b. -downwards, and it is obvious that users of these weights are meeting with much difficulty in supplying their wants. Repairers' bends sell readily, and are account' ing for a big aggregate of middle-weights. Stoxit, shoulders are greatly needed, supported by steady demand for the lighter selections, but the demand for bellies remain quiet. -

Rough straps go very strongly, belting mann- lecturers being hard put-to, obtaining quiric and sufficient delivery. owing to the large mink' her of. these butts going out for other thar strapping purposes. Dressing shoulders easill Maintain their very strong position, and for- ther contracts are easily placed as offered. Hides are now fairly uniform at the official limits, one or• two markets have t yet collo into line, due mainly to individual " cussed- ness," and it is rumoured that, failing speedy realisation of the position, drastic actioo will be taken with them.—" Leather Trade° Review."

MUSIC AND THE PEOPLES

Bands' Association Presenta- tion to Mr. J. A. Dawson.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
APPRECIATED.

An interesting meeting took place at the Town BAB on Monday evening, when a clock and two bronze statuettes were presented as a wedding 'gift to Mr. J. A. Dawson, secretary of the War- rington Bands' Association. Mr. James Lee (president of the Association) oceuvied the chair.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PRESIDENT'S PRAISE.

Mr. Lee, in the course of 'a short opening speech, said they were gathered together in order to pay tribute to their worthy secretary, Mr. Dawson. (Applause.) Mr.. Dawson had worked very strenuously for the Bands' Associa- tion—work which had not always been of the pleasantest kind—and now, when he thought it advisable to take. to himself a wife, they seized the opportunity to show their appreciation of his services. The small attendance was due no doubt to the long hours Which some of the wen were now working, but he was sure the •tribute extended to Mr. Dawson would be none .the less sincere. (Hear, hear.) They were pleased to have with them the Mayor to make .the presentation. Alderman Peacock had done a, great deal for the town and if he (the speaker) Was a judge, he had done a=little for the Bands' Association. (Hear, hear.) Although his Wor- ship had not been able, perhaps, to do all he desired to do for them he had done his best, and the results they appreciated to the full. (Ap- . plause.) Some people might say that this Was not a time for the making of presentations. Perhaps the times were not the best, but They could not put off a man's wedding until the war was over. It was the unanimous wish 'of The Bands' Association that the presentation should be made, and that although the times -were abnormal, they should Spay this small tribute to their secretary; and they made the -present with a good heart. (Hear, hear.) They were especially obliged for His Worship's presence at their little gathering when they knew of the many calls he had upon his time and energy. It was really wonderful how the "Mayor Was able to do so much. (Applause.) THE MAYOR AND WORK IN GOOD CAUSES.

The Mayor, who was then asked by Mr. Lee to make the presentation on behalf of the Association and was received with applause, asked Mr. Dawson's acceptance of a clock and pair of bronzes, subscribed for. by the various hands in Warrington, in recognition of his ser- 'vices to their Association. (Applause.) The members knew ..Mr. Dawson had worked for them as an AssoLation, and that 'much of the success which had attended' their efforts had been due to him. (Hear, hear.) An occasion upon which they could express their thanks With better grace than at the time Mr: Dawson had taken this good lady for his wife had not 'arisen and was. not likely to arise. (Hear, hear.) His personal relations with Mr. Dawson tended over a period of 21 years, in business .and other ways, and he had always found that if he could help on a good cause, without get- ting anything for it, he was only too delighted -to do it. He and Mr. Dawson had lived long enough to learn that many people put their best 'in associations and other good causes, only to =et kicked at the end for their pains. BARRACKS CONCERTS.

Speeches And Music To Honour Departing Comrades.

In honour of Sergeant-Major Corbett (of 'the 3rd Battalion South Lancashire Regiment) and a few sergeants who took their departure to the battalion at Thornton, a smoking concert was held early in the week in the Sergeants' Mess, Orford Barracks. Sergeant-Major Quick took the chair, and the committee consisted ,of Company-Sergeant-Majors Newall and Camp- bell, assisted by Quartermaster-Sergeant Stephens. Sergeant-Major Quick in his speech wished all the departing comrades the best of luck and a safe return covered with honours. (Hear, hear.) The toast was then drunk.

Sergeant-Major Corbett shortly leaves for the Front, and in a short but hearty speech, re- marked how sorry he was to have to leave so many friends and conclude his happy times at the Barracks—although, of course, he did not repine at going to do his duty. • Mn. Harry Ashtonwho was among the civilians present—made a fine speech, touching upon the glorious traditions of the South Lan- cashire. Regiment, not only in the present, but also in past wars. He was greeted with loud applause. Mr. 80x,.. recently returned from the Dar- danelles, also spoke, mentioning how glad he was to be home again amongst so many friends.

The programme—which was voted "Al" by all present—commenced with the favourite old ballad "Sally in our alley," given in style by Company-Sergeant-.Major Campbell. Messrs. Wilde and Woodhouse (of Warrington) followed with concertina and piano selections, which earned loud applause. After Sergeant Crouch's song, "The Convict," had been given, Sergeant Tinton recited "The Dandy Fifth" with suc- cess. Dr. Hughes carried his audience in first- class manner with "Marguerite," and in re- sponse to an encore obliged with "The Blind Boy." Schoolmaster Connors then gave that splendid Scotch song, "Of a' the airts that winds can Wow," and Sergeant Leadbetter fol- 16wed with a much appreciated series of mouth-organ selections, Sergeant Mitchell (of the R.P.A.) having sung "Sound as a bell," the interval came round.

Upon the resumption, Mr. Wilde led off with a rousing .concertina solo, and Sergeant Wil- liams maintained the spirit with a humorous item, "The Inquisitive Kiddie." The next Highland selection- came from Sergt. Howard (of the Loyal North Lancashire Regiment) in "Bonnie Mary of Argyle." Sergeant-Major Quick "brought the house down" with "I love a lassie," and as an encore gave "The Pride of the Line." Following. Sergeant Lead- better's clever card-trick performance, the singing of "Auld hang .syne" and the National Anthem concluded a capital evening.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
A SECOND CONCERT.

Splendid Prouranime Hugely Enjoyed

On Thursday:

Another grand concert was given in the Gymnasium at Orford Barracks on Thursday evening. There was a good attendance of soldiers and friends, and Sergeant-Major Quick was in the chair. As a preliminary, a few items were given by some of the soldiers, one of whom was a Colonial, and they were thoroughly enjoyed. A splendid programme of items by well-known local artistes was then entered upon, opening with a duet entitled "In Springtime," artistically sung by Mr. 'and Mrs. Sephton. Miss Perdue then rendered in fine style two songs with chorus, "The King's Own" and "Under the British Flag." In response to a double encore, the lady gave "Mine's Sniders !" Miss Mabel Hewitt gracefully danced the "Scottish Reel" and the ' Irish Jig." The Brooklyn Quartet Party gave some good selec- tions, included in which were "Where Love Lies Dreaming," "The Old Brigade," and "Tally Ho." Mrs. Sephton delighted all by her rendering of the song "I Heard a Voice," while Miss Elsie Hewitt was heard to great advantage in her rendering of "Heart's Delight." Later she took part in a duet entitled "Excelsior," along with Mr. W. H. Rigby. Mr. Sephton sang expressively and with fine spirit, "The Veteran's Song." Mr. Rigby scored a notable success in "Up From Zummer- zet," which gave plenty of scope for his, excel- lent voice.'Mr. W. Hopkins fairly "brought the house down " with his humorous songs, "Thumbs Up" and "I Followed Her Here," and in response to encores he gave 'lt's a Long, Long Walk," and an imitation of a cats' mid- night concert, concluding with a humorous description of a cat. The duties of pianist were ably carried out by Mr. A. E. Prow. The concert over, Sergeant-Major Quick, in proposing a hearty vote of thanks to the artistes, said he was sure all had spent a most enjoyable evening. In his opinion the concert given that evening was one of the finest that had ever taken place in the barracks. Great thanks were due to the -artistes for so kindly giving their services that evening. He then asked for three cheers for the artistes, which were heartily given.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

CLAYTON-EVANS.

On Thursday, at Elmwood Avenue Congrega- tional Church, the marriage took place of Mr. Harry L. Clayton, son of Mr. and Mrs. John Clayton,. Padgate-lane, and Miss Winifred S. Evans, eldest daughter of Mr. F. Evans, Hamil- ton-street, the ceremony being conducted by the Rev. John Yonge. The bride, who was given away by her father, woie a dress of ivory silk crepe-de-chene, trimmed with true lovers' knots of pearls, also a cap and veil with pearls and orange blossom. She carried a sheaf of Madonna lilies.

The bridesmaids, the Misses Marie„and Doris Evans '(sisters of the bride) were prettily attired in embroidered voile, with white felt hats trimmed with shell pink silk. They carried shower bouquets of pink sweet peas. Mr. Frank Dutton, of Paddington, acted as best man.

The reception was held in the guild room adjoining the church, the happy couple leaving later in the day for Llandudno. Mr. and Mrs. Clayton were recipients of numerous presents.

Daughter Of Munitions Minister Returns To France.

Miss Olwen. Lloyd George, elder daughter of the Minister of Munitions, left Victoria by the boat express ,on Thursday afternoon to return to France, where she has been engaged as a Red Cross nurse. Miss Lloyd George, who is returning to duty after leave of absence in Lon- don, was accompanied to Victoria Station by Mts. Lloyd George, Miss Megan- Lloyd George, and a number of lady friends.

comfort of .our wounded sailors and soldiers, however trifling, deserves consideration. It has been suggested that this year's,lavender should be kept for use in hospitals. The flowers- should be rubbed from the stalks and the latter be cut in even lengths, and with thread tied in bundles of six or eight. The blossoms should be placed in muslin, or thin silk bags, and laid among the hospital sheets. The stalks when burnt and waved about re- fresh and purify the atmosphere of 'sick wards. Women who live in the country, and are un- able to give other service, might help the wounded by preparing the -lavender, and by sending bunches- of sweet-smelling kerbs, such as rosemary, balm,. mint, and sage, to the hospitals in their own neighbourhood. Such posies would be hig3ly appreciated by the men and their nurses. • Fashionable Furs. •

Autumn fashions, are now definitely decided: but .the future will prove which, and -how many, of the new -modes' will become general. Although winter seems- a: long way off yet, it is by no means too early to discuss what• furs will be worn when the colic weather comes. It is safe to say that .4 note of simplicity and re- straint will be a feature of the furrier's pro- ductions this season, for liar and costly peltry savours too much of ostentatfon to be tolerated at the Present time. Sables; tfiterefore, will not be much in evidence. Skunk will be among the most popular of furs. It is comparatively cheafi, and, undyed, has 'a rfch, -dark brown colour that is very' becoming. Wkite skunk is a novelty that will probably .find favour, on account of its cheapness and gaud appearance. It would be better described. as yellow than white, and is flecked liberally wiiffr dark hairs.

Black For Afternoon And Evening Wear. Black was never more universally worn than at present; women who are not in in,qirning elect to wear it, not only for o...ening, but for smart afternoon 'frocks. Dressmakers, in order to avoid monotony and-to obtain novel effects and distinctive styles, are using as many as four different materials in the nulling of a single dress. Thus satin, velvet, tulle and jet are often combined, but great discretion is 'required in the mingling of the different fabrics, or the result is far from successful. Passementerie is .used again, and applique will have "a vogue in the autumn. Crystal is very much in evidence for the trimming of black evening dresses. Corsages are only slightly, decollete,

incentive to healthy employment, it is, no mat- ter of surprise if by the time children are men and women they have learnt to avoid irksome tasks and have become one 'of those folks who are continually on the look-out for something new. This is not really to no wondered at, as we are naturally creatures of habit, and the question as to whether they be good or bad ones is a matter which is to a great extent in the hands of the mother.

Regular programmes of work and play should be Made, and this should be strictly.kept. Do not change children's work or play at the cap- rice of the moment. If you do you will siinply dis hearten them, and they will not be efficient in anything, but get into the way of thinking that matters can be shelved at will. On tile other hand, do not force your children to do any- thing they really dislike. A great dear can be done by tactfulness and a child taught' to. over- come any little fad or fancy.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

Modern idolatry. A portentous sign of the times is the preva- lence among all classes of a belief in the power of amulets, mascots, charms and talismans, to protect their owners or wearers from evil. This belief would seem to point to a return to mediceval superstition following upon a decay of religious faith.- The Russian soldier goes into battle wearing his ikon, and praying devoutly; the French soldier carries his cruci- fix. Both are symbols of faith' and dependence on Divine protection. The Irish Catholic regiments carry their crucifix. and rosary, and bend the kneee in prayer for a few moments before charging the enemy.. But British sol- diers more generally appear to carry sipme kind of mascot, for which, on account of the war, there is quite a craze. Nothing short of this could surely account for the unpreceden:ed sale of one popular charm called Touchwood. This Eastern mascot originated; it is said, in the veneration of relics of the Holy Cross.

Honours For French Peasant Women. The French Minister of Agriculture has noti- fied his intention to reward the peasant women of France; who, after the mobilisation of the men, saved by their efforts and labours the food supply of the French nation. The reward will take the. form of Crox de ,Guerre, and many thousands will be distributed when the war is over. The war crosses will be either the Cross of Agricultural Merit, or the Medal of Encouragement. The former is made of enamel and is suspended on a 'green and red ribbon; the latter is of bronze and has the tricolour for its ribbon. Thanks to the women the price of bread in France is normal. With- out the slightest hesitation, at the outbreak of hostilities, the peasant women took the com- mand ef the farms, followed the plough, at- tended to the livestock, and are now reaping the harvests they saved in the Spring. Their unostentatious and splendid work deserves the distinction, that will be al/ the more highly Prized since this is the first time it has been conferred upon women.

HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes and Useful Suggestions.

Local Dishes.

It is possible to have tea in some wayside cottage or small farmhouse in Cumberland and see fifteen different kinds of home-made cake on the table. In Devonshire the fisherfolk have many delicious ways of cooking fish. Golden bream in particular is excellent if stuffed With an ordinary veal stuffing and baked with slices of bacon laid acrossnt; and the same way of cooking may be applied to pollock or fresh haddock. To people of the North, Lancashire hot-pot and Irish stew (which used to be called locally " lobscousy'e') are better than those made in other districts, and Yorkshire cooking is, of its kind, perhaps the best in the world. This collecting and making known the cooking customs of different parts of our own country might also serve to wear down the prejudice against foreign ways, for it would show, how much affinity there really is between English and foreign customs. The Dutch habit of eat- ing cheese to currant bread would not seem so strange to English people when they know the same thing is done in Lancashire and York- shire. The very identical sweet milk bread

that is the speciality of a certain town in Southern Europe can be found in a Devonshire village, and Italian polenta may pass muster as a variation on Scotch porridge.—" Tele- graph." Rice With Cheese.

Let boil, for a few minutes only a quarter of a pound of good Carolina rice which has been ' previously well washed and soaked. Take up, strain, let cool. Put into saucepan with a lump of fresh butter and some meat gravy, salt and pepper. Let cook gently till tender. Add quarter of a pound of grated cheese, stirring • carefully till perfectly mixed. Take up and serve very hot. Brown Onion Soup. Required: Three pints of stock, 141 b. of onions, 2 leeks, 12 peppercorns, 2 cloves, a bay 'leaf, a sprig of thyme, 2oz. of ham, „pz. of brown flour, a teaspoonful of sugar, some browning, and half a carrot. Cut the ham in slices, put it with the dripping in a frying-pan; when hot add the onions 'arid leeks „t sliced, ,the carrots, also sliced, and the susar. Stir over the' fire till brown,' pour in the not stock, boil,. skim, add the spices and flavourings, boil to a pulp. Pass all through a wire sieve, return it to the pan with the thickening and colouring, and salt to taste; add stock to thin the soup , somewhat if too thick. Serve with or without grated cheese.,

Poached Eggs And Cheese. Poached eggs make a nice luncheon d'ish served with cheese, thus: Put fresh butter and grated cheese in a baking dish. Let them melt in the oven. Poach the eggs, and set on the cheese. Serve at once; or have slices of hot buttered toast, peppered with grated cheese, ready, upon which to Fay the poached eggs. Helpful Children. The best time to train children for responsi- bility, says "Maud," in "Farm and Home," is when they are tiny tots, and perhaps the most natural commencement when they have finished with their toys to insist that they are put away tidily. To do this does not mean be- coming irritable about the matter if the chil- dren are not particularly anxious to do so, as if you have made it a rule to wait on them hand and foot they will consider it a hardship, at first, to do anything at all for themselves and to relieve them of any task "just for once, because you are anxious to go out or do some- thing else, is an unwise proceeding. FREE, WHEN YOUNG.

But, as an Old Man, the Bachelor is Usually

Forlorn.

The world is full of foolish bachelors, says a writes. They are running around. spending thekr money in all sorts of profitless ways, when they would be much better off spending it in supporting a wife and family. As-bachelors they have nothing to show for-the money spent.. As married men they would have a wife, hone and family.. As a young man the bachelor is tree from rghonsibility and at libtrty to spend all he:earn-upon hifaself. But as an, older man he is:siinply. forlorn. Most men seem to have an idea that they can marry any woman at any age. It is .a mistaken ' idea, however. It may be possible fax any man to marry, but it is not possible for him to pick and choose, especially after he is an unattractive old bachelor. A man should marry between the ages of 25 and 35. After the latter age ,he is beginning to get crabbed and fussy in his ways. There is a great deal of talk about old maids I being fussy and prim in their ways, but as a matter of fact they are not half as much so as old bachelors. The married man makes a ffn better citizen than the bachelor. The points, at issue in the town's government are of more vital interest to him. The bachelor is -.selfish,, thinking only of himself and his own pleasures: The married man spends his life working for others. All that is best in him is developed and broadened, lwhile all that is best in the bachelor is cramped and narrowed. The married man mellb.ws with age, the bachelor grows crusty or. fbolish. There is no more forlorn sight- than a tonely old bachelor. No matter how well'offlie is, he gets a neglected shabby appearance. An ord maid can usually gather a few householtk gOods about her, and make a cosy nook for herself, even if her only home'he a ball bedroom. But the bachelor lives in a state of chronii.l.rertieliness and discomfort.

The Comfoirtabl.e Smock.

Smocking has come prominently to the fore again, and with lacing forms one of the chief features of autumn shirts and blouses. One of the most useful garments, as well as the most comfortable is, the smock, which is having the vogue it deserves,. Made for summer wear of pique or holland,. it frequently had a honey- comb yoke, and was worn with a striped skirt. For winter, models, shown .are of light woollen materials, and in shape resemble the smock worn universally by farm labourers during the 19th century. It is- very easily and quickly made, but must be well cut. One variation reaches the knees, has big pockets on either side, and a turnover collar of white lawn. The sleeves are of the Raglan shape, long and quite full, and at the wrist are smocked to match the front and back, a frill falling over the hand. Sometimes the corsage has a crossover effect, for there are many varietli,s Qf tliiS garment. What Next?

Women are supposed. to.' do most af the war economising, and little is-heard about the man doing without his pipe or cigar. Challenged in this respect he is apt to say that smoking helps to pay for the war, seeing that out of every shilling he spends on tobacco 'at. least tenpence goes to the Government for duty.. The rest, however, goes abroad, and not raw& progress has been made as yet in producing. home-grown tobacco. Some of the dress- economists, apparently inspired by the. " no hat" fashion, are suggesting that we might do. without ex- pensive boots, and Use .sandals- for footwear. There was an idiotic craze for sandals a few years back, but nearly all its devotees soon came to the conclusion after trying. them, that sandals were More suited .for children than adults. As regards going hatless, most. of the women and girls seen in the streets- without head-covering are Belgians. In Beliüm and in many French towns .liats are regarded as be- longing to "best clothes," and are not un- commonly dispensed with for workaday pur- poses by women and girls 14 the middle classes.

HINTS ON • HEALTH.

Useful Information In Popular Language.

Control Of Rheumatic Fever.

Rheumatic attacks in' children are very. easily overlooked as the outward s'ymptoms are usually quite trifling, and, says a leaflet issued, by the County Borough of Croydon, likely to escape the notice of parents. At the same time, these slight attacks , may permanently damage a child's heart and• produce very serious results in later life. Hence it is very important that treatment should be secured immediately for rheumatism in its earliest stages and mildest forms. Precautions that should be takers with rheumatic children:—(l) Keep limbs anti body warmly but lightly clothed, preferably with woollen underclothing. (2) Put child., to bed, and obtain medical advice whenever feverish- ness, growing pains, sore throat, or other symp- toms of rheumatism are noticed. (3) Eiffarged tonsils are better removed if a child' suffers from repeated sore throat.

The Need for Domestic Hygiene Just'Now. In a trenchant article in July. " Go'od-Health" on the subject of "Hygiene in the- Home," 'Marie Blanche gives the following, timely advice: "In warm weather flies will always congregate where exposed refuse lies,-and disease germs are often carried into the house-this way. It is, therefore, the duty of every householder and every housewife to considEr domestic hygiene, not alone for the safety of their house- hold, but for the good of the community at Dirge. It would be a terrible scourge if some devastating epidemic were to break out and sweep over our country at the present time, crowded as it is with vast camps and hundreds of wounded soldiers. Trained nurses and medical men and women might be Inone too plentiful, for enormous numbers of' them have gone across the Channel and are likely to be wanted there while the war lasts. If such a catastrophe as an epidemic of typhoid or typhus were to overtake England now, there might searcely be nurses enough to go- round. The gravity of the consequences can hardly be ex- aggerated." Alcohol.

A diminution in the consuinption, of alcohol by the community would mean less tuberculosis, less poverty, less dependency, less pressure on our hospitals, asylums and gaola. Intemperate drinking cuts into the support of the family The drinking of parents weakens the vitality of children. Drinking mothers lose twiee- as many babies as do sober mothers Mar, alcoholism is found in the parents of faebl?- minded children than in tlfe parents of normal children. The children of drinkers de..O6P more slowly and do ponrer school wdrk- than do the children of abstainers. Alcohol impairs , the tone of the muscles and lessens the product of labourers; it depreciates the skill' and endurance of artisans; it impairs memory, multiplies industrial accidents, causes ddroni.! disease of the heart, liver, stomach and kidney,: increases the death rate,ftem pneumonia, lessens natural immunity to infectious diseases;--De. IEATH OF THE REV. 3: CARSON:

The Rev. James Carson, rector of Newton-in- Makerfield, died on Tuesday and was buried on Thursday at Wargrave amid many manifesta- tions of sorrow. Ile was ordained deacon in Ireland in 1857, and made priest in 1858. He was curate of Donnybrook, County Dublin, from 1857 to 1864,, and of Adam, County Limerick, from 1864-7, becoming Teeter and vicar of Clog- lane, County Kerry, in 1867. in 1872 he began his work in England', his first appointment in this country being to the curacy of Ironbridge, Salop, in 1872. Two years later he became' curate of Neen SaVage, &fop, He came to Lan- cashire in 1877 to take up a ouracy at Winwick. In 1892 he was appointed Rector of Newton-in- Makerfield.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
"A VENTURE OF FAITH."

War Anniversary And Church Processions..

The Warrington Parish Church- Magazine says:— " Wednesday, August 4th, was kept in, our parish, as doubtless in many others, as a day of prayer and intercession in connection with the war. The day began with a Celebration of the Holy Communion in the Parish Church and. both Missions, and from eight in the morning, to nine in the evening parishioners knelt in

prayer for a quarter of an hour or more to ask. God's pardon for our sins, and help and blessing. in this crisis of the world's history. At -eight a procession of the clergy, choirs and .congre- gations of the two Missions started from St. Georges, and traversing the streets of the two districts of St. George's and St. Clement's, ended with. a solemn little service at St. Clement's. On the way stops were made in two places, and addresses given by thb clergy, litanies and hymns being sung on the march. It was a very striking little demonstration, received with sympathetic appreciation by the people, who listened attentively to the addresses and joined heartily in the singing." I should like to offer a kindly word of _ _ _ _ .

thanks to the choir and other § who- took part in the, outdoor procession on August 4th," says the writer of the St. Clement's notes in the magazine. "It was a venture of faith, but I think it exceeded all our expectations. In my parish-visiting since, I have found many signs that the prayers and hymns brought home a message to those who lived in the surrounding streets. The present moment is undoubtedly the time to carry the Gospel Message out into the streets, in order to bring home to others the fact that the war is a call to the nation to return to God."

ESSAY THIS WEEK: "WHAT I THINK 0

THE WAR."

MY DEAR CHILDREN,-

After our Drawing Competition we no return to the weekly essay, which this time want you to pen upon ".What I think of th war." Now, of course lam aware that yo', think a lot about the war. Everybody, in fact„ has heaps and heaps of ideas on it—often I wish people hadn't; or, at least, Would not talk about them! I get sometimes'qiiite tired of listening to the really remarkable things which they say. Sometimes, too, I get quite downhearted when I hear them tell how poor old England is "on its last legs" because no- body will make Mr. So-and-So the Prime Minis- ter, and Mr. What's-his-Name the Chancellor. Mr. So-and-So and Mr. What's-his-Name, it would appear, have exclusive right of what has been called the milk of Paradise—a wonderful ,thing to have, although nobody seems to know what it is. _ _

But on the other hand I am very anxious in- deed to hear what you have got to say about the war; because, of course, all Uncle Ned's nephews and nieces know how to talk sense. Indeed, so eager am I to know your views that. just for once, you may (if you wish) write a little longer letter than usual. But it must not be much longer, mind; and / am only allowing it this once because I know how very hard it is to put one's war-ideas briefly. , So let us have a capital entry, as usual! You can talk about kings, or shoes or ships or sealing wax, or the pink forms or the pale faces, or the war fun*, or " Punch " cartoons or anything you like. Only deal gently, please, with the Cabinet Ministers; they have enough to put up with as it is, and I love them. Closing Day: Monday.

i F m I ke ,u

PARISH CHURCH INNOVATfON.

First and Third Sundays Choir And Clergy Will "Go Out."

The. Warrington Parish Churlll Magazine for September makes the following announce- ment..—

"We desire to draw the attention of our people to a little change which. we proposo to makeiaa the nature and order of the 10.30 morn- ing services at the harvest festival on the first Sunday in October. There will be Choral Matins, as on the second and fourth Sundays until the hymn at the third Collect, then will follow, the sermon. _

"During the next hymn there will be the usual collection, and after the presentation of the alms and the blessing the choir and clergy will go out. At 11.15 the choral celebration will begin as a distinct and separate service without a sermon, the offertory taking place at the appointed time. This will continue to be our use on the first and tliird Sundays."

Sir Charles Nlacara On German

Troubles.

Sir Charles Macara, discussing in Tilawfaester on Thursday, a report that in Germany cotton was selling at 3s. a lb., said that was more than five times its normal value, and must I awe the effect of abolishing cotton spinning-. The stop- page of cotton supplies had been olfe lf the heaviest blows we had been able to inflict upon our enemi6s.

As for the suggestion that rags were being substituted for cotton in the manufacture of explosives, it was obvious that the supply of rags must soon be exhausted. All .tatements about changes of methods of manufacture of explosives Should be accepted with. reserve.
