THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 4, 1915.

A PRACTICAL SUGGESTION

Mr. Lowther, the Speaker of the House' of Commons, has been making a practical speech on the thrift question. Thrift, he evidently thinks, should, like charity, begin at 'home. He suggef3ts, for instance, that the State, which is relying upon its individual members to practise thrift, 'should first set the ex-

ample by discontinuing the salaries Of mem- bers of Parliament for tho period of the war. He thinks the State might also reduce the number of its officials. As to officials, we should be disposed to think that the number of enlistments has reduced the staffs in Gov- ernment offices, while the needs of the war have increased the work to be done, and that reform in this department is not so urgent as in peace time. In the case of members of Parliament, the argument appears to be all the other way. They have little .or no public work to do, as war is the only business of our rulers now, and war cannot be conducted by Parliament as a whole, but by a small selected body working in its name. Their energies will be largely diverted to other occupations, and not a few of them are sorting with the forces—and we presume being paid directly and indirectly by way of allowances to depend- ents—so that there is a case for saving the quarter of a million sterling the nation pays them for the work they are not now doings When we are being asked to save our crumbs., the State might well save its quarter millions. ,

===== 0002601_19150904_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCIL SAVINGS.

The Town Council have been asked by the Local Government Beard to exercise strict economy in every branch of expenditure, whether capital or revenue, urging the post- ponement of work to be borne by private in- dividuals unless urgently necessary, and re- questing that the Board may be supplied with teperts from time -to time the nature and

extent of any savings which local authoritie4 might have been able to effect.. A copy of the letter has been sent to each member of the Council and to the head of each department. It has been decided to apply for an Order extending the time for the construction of the river wall, Knutsford-road, the completion of Chester-road, and other works under the War- rington Corporation Act, 1911. Besides, it is recommended by the Trams Committee, "that in order to encourage the saving of petrol, the motor 'bus drivers be paid a bonus of is. per week when they average six 'miles to the gallon, and that an additional bonus of is. per week be paid when the average is 64 miles or over."

===== 0002601_19150904_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
MEDICAL INSPECTION.

There is no mention in the Health Com- mittee's minutes, to be submitted to the Town Council on Tuesday night, of the crisis that has arisen in the Medical Inspection Sub- committee of the Education Committee. We suppose the next move rests with the Educa- tion Committee, and in the meantime it may be considered that silence will be golden.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR LADY POSTMEN.

Experience, brief as it has been, has shown that the young women, who have so bravely undertaken to do the work of the postmen, so that the shortage of male labour may be felt as little as possible, are acquitting themselves admirably. • The way in which some of them lend themselves to the work certainly give one the impression that they think the issue of the day hangs upon the way in which they perform their individual duties. That is the spirit that should animate us all, and does animate most of us. We shall win this war when we have taken it, and our share in it, whatever that may be, seriously, and we do not know a better object lesson in this re- spect than the one provided by our lady post- men. By the way, is lady postman the cor- rect name for these new and useful public servants? Postwoman does not sound Well, though familiarity might make it sound less harsh.

On the minutes of the Tramways Committee appears the following entry :

An application from the Postmaster to allow, during the period of war, postmen and post. women to break their journey on the tramcars, for the purfioses of collection and deliyery of letters, was read, and it was resolved that dur- ing the period of the war the manager be authorised to make the necessary arrangements in order to facilitate postal work.

We are pleased to note that the concession has been made. These are days for lending a helping hand in every direction—even to a municipal department assisting a Government department.

FOOTBALL NEXT SATURDAY.

The Warrington Football Club enter upon their winter's programme next Saturday, when the first of the junior matches, which are to provide entertainment weekly at Wilde,rspool, will be played. It is hoped that members of the club will assist the committee to make a success of this war-time stop-gap effort to keep the flag flying, while at the same time they comply with the needs of the situation 13,,,v refusing to allow any distraction that would necessarily be caused by senior football. A *lilted membership fee is to be charged, and if the well-wishers of the club will, take advantage of the. concession in lard numbers the situation, present and future, will be assured. The age limit for players has been fixed at eighteen.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
NEEDS OF THE INFIRMARY.

It will have been noticed that the Infirmary authorities have since the war began been very modest in their appeals to the public for financial support. The strain which the in- stitution has to bear has been none the less; in fact, war-time prices of provisions and labour have added materially to the cost of maintenance, and, naturally, the treasurer has some anxious moments. There was a debit balance at the beginning of the year of £1,219 45., which, it is estimated, will be in- creased by December 31st to £1,607 7s. May we ask our readers, in the plenitude of their generosity—upon which the various funds make a constant demand—not to forget the zieeds of the -Infirmary, which plods along with the same old fidelity to the poor of the town as ever? As will be seen from our ad- vertising columns, the special Infirmary War Fund has now reached £1,915. Thirty beds are available for military patients, and this without an encroachment having been made upon the demands of the civil population. The Infirmary is a case for the liberal con- sider:44olrd fill wino are in ti position to give.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 4, 1915.

THE

DEPRECIATED SOVEREIGN.

How We Can Help To Restore

Full Value For £l.

EXCHANGE INTRICACIES.

BY C. E. PARKER.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—The subject of international payments involving rates of exchange is ordinarily con- sidered OP technical to be understood, except by experts; but its importance at the present hour is so great that we ought all to 'try and understand it. It presents no .real difficulty. The differences of exchange arise from our different measures of money. In America the dollar is the measure; in this country- the sovereign. Roughly the usual relation is five dollars to one sovereign, or to he -nore exact a little under five, 4.86. This relation is always subject to slight variation according to the demand for the one or the other to make payments. Our increaso imports from; and our decreased exports to America has brought about an unusual depreciation in the value of the sovereign when measured by the dollar, and at present if you desire to convert a sovereign into dollars you will only get 4.53 dollars in- stead of 4.86. This means a difference of seven per cent.; that is; to buy a Sovereign's worth of American goods measured by the dollar you only get eighteen and sevenpence worth. We do not send sovereigns for all we buy. The great bulk of our transactions is settled by bills and cheques, and is cleared or balanced by cross transactions. But this makes no difference in our loss when the exchange is against us, for the conversion of values be- tween the two countries' coinage has to be made in every transaction on the basis of the ruling rates of exchange.

Loan Inadvisable; Gold Limited. It has been repeatedly suggested that we ought to float a loan in America, a loan which would necessarily have to be arranged on better terms than our present War Loan. But there is a strong objection to this, because the investment Market is international, and English buyers Would have 'no difficulty in par- ticipating. The immediate effect would be to depreciate the value of our War Loan and the old Consuls and this Would not be fair to our home investors, and would•he a breach of faith of the conditions under which the last loan was floated. It might be possible, although difficult, to restrict the loan to those domiciled in America, and thus make it free of the British income tax. But even this would be objectionable, as it wolild lessen the possible proceeds from ta,xes, so necessary at the pre- sent hour.- The diffieulties of floating a special American loan are more serious than people ordinarily consider.. If there is no other way of liquidating our debt to America it will have to be paid in gold. If we could pa); in gold it would be the best way to correct the exchange. No doubt a considerable amount of gold could he released for this purpose from„domestic use by a more .general use of paper inoney, and we all should hear this in mind. We should, however, soon reach the limit of the amount of gold we could safely afford to export.

Exports to Pay. Another method, in which many, if not all, can help, is to do all that is possible to lessen our requirements from America, and at the same time, and what amounts to the same thing, increase our exports. The bulk of what comes ,i.from America we cannot do without. Looking over our list of imports it is impossible to lay one's finger on anything large we do not re- quire. The question resolves itself into one of economy in the use of these imports. And this in its turn involves economy all round, because what we can save on our homo products and manufactures results in less demand from abroad to supply our shortage. Hence it seems purposeless to advise an individual to attempt to discriminate in his economies between goods imported and goods produced at home. Economy in home products is as necessary as economy in imports. This 'point is very similar to the ques- tion raised as to the merits of saving to invest in the War Loan, or, say, saving to lodge with the Warrington Savings Bank. ,The effect is the same: one is direct, the other attains the same end by a more circuitous route.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
Work and Save.

The desirability of increasing our exports to America to pay for our excess of imports is the most important in considering the question of the adverse exchange. Here, of course, we are doubly handicapped. Our workers are reduced by the, national demands, by those who have enlisted, and by the labour transferred to make' war material. In view of the dislocation of in- dustry, it is surprising that-our exports have kgpt up so well. The question which each one would address to himself "Can I do Anything to assist in in- creasing these exports?" And here again it is unnecessary to discriminate between labour on goods for export or for home consumption. The variation forms of *ork are so interdependent that the man who works'to increase the produc- tion of articles for home use is indirectly assist- ing in the manufacture of goods for export. It thus becomes a question of individual duty that everyone should save all he can, invest it safely and where he may, and he should do all the work he can in the production of useful articles. He should not alone save wealth, but should also strive to make it. Everyone should do his bit, and this should be as large a bit as he can possibly make it; he must serve by working as well as serve by saving. Penketh, September 2nd, 1915.

LATEST WAR NEWS.

AMSTERDAM, Friday

It is officially announced from Berlin that the town of Grodno has been taken by troops belonging to Von Hindenburg's group and 400 prisoners made in street fighting.

PARIS, Thursday (delayed) The Petrograd correspondent of the "Temps" quotes the "Russkoie Slovo" 'for a Tokio message that Japan has offered to send troops to Europe.

HAVRE, Friday., A Belgian official communique reports a vio- lent bomb duel before Diamude yesterday, in which the Belgians gained the upper hand.

Zußrcn, Friday. A Bucharest message to the "Munchener Neueste Nachrichten" announces the imminent conclusion of negotiations between Bulgaria and Roumania, the latter promising substan- tial rectification of frontier.—Central News.

HELP AND ADVICE FOR ALL.

SPECIAL BY "THE

I spent a few hours in Parr Hall on Wednes- day in order to prove by personal contact the amount of good work that I had heard was being done there .in the way of helping those who are most in need of help at the present time—those who are suffering by the war in any way.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PERMANENT STAFF.

, Mr. T. Spinks, the assistant town clerk, act- ing fort Mr. J. Lyon Whittle, is the Presiding genius over this establishment, and he and his staff—Mr. H. Lawton (Town's Clerk's Depart- ment), Mr. Mason ,and Mr. Whittle (Gas Department) and Mr.. Gaulter (Surveyor's Department), live to make easy the path of those in need of help. As trouble after trouble was laid before Mr. Spinks and as he sifted the evidence each one had to give, he reminded mf) somewhat of a revising barrister, judicial but kindly, considering the applications from every point of view, and then deciding upon a certain line of action. But the result, instead of being a vote was kindly advice, leading in many cases to the banishment of anxiety.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
The Lost Book And Promoted Husband.

While Mr. Spinks was considering an applica- tion from a woman who had lost her book and could not get her pay at the Post Office, he was called out into the corridor by, someone who required him.' After a moment's disappearance he returned. "It is," he said, "a young Dutchman want- ing work "—an indication of the varied nature of the requirements to which the office has to attend.

The lost book trouble was promptly made all right, temporarily. _

_ . -The third caller was a widow whose sons had "joined up." She was living with her daugh- ter now, she said, but her daughter was married and could not afford to keep her. Satisfied with the claim, Mr. Spinks told the old lady the sum to which she was entitled, promised to make all the necessary. arrange- ments, and, what was of more immediate con- cern, added: "We will keep you going for the present, and passed her on to the neighbouring room, where the Sailors' and 'Soldiers' -Associa- tion workers are engaged Daying oiut grants in proper cases. That temporary arrangement will last until an overworked, diad therefore slow-working War Office, moves. To new claimants the advice was given that the claims must be made "within a month." or the War Office will not be moved either by, heaven or earth.

• "Will you put me in the way of getting my extra pay?" said the next applicant. "He has been made Sergeant since June 2nd, and my pay is still 'the same."' "how do you know he has been made Sergeant ?"

The woman produced the necessary informa- tion. •

"How ninny children have you?"

" Three."

"Then you ar entitled to 255. gd. instead of 23,4.... I will write for you, and where do you live? . . . If You don't hear anything in the course of ten days come down to see me again." And another mind was mode easy by the knowledcze that justice would not be allowed to cep bY default ,for lack of help by those who know how to give it. Startling Requests.

The next case was of a different type. It was an undeserving case and as much firmness was shown .in dealing with it as kindness and consideration had been shown in the others. There is at the Parr Hall, as may be surmisea, a "black list," but therec.are disreputable characters in all classes; and it would be a miracle indeed if all who pass through such an office as this, where help of every kind may be had for the asking, if everyone who asked was immaculate.

Many and varied—and sometimes startling— are the requests that are made in the course of a day. A mother with a missing son has so much confidence in this universal provider of good cheer that she seeks the help of the ,office in finding him. His name is the only aid she can supply and she hardly realises that this office that has done so much cannot trace his whereabouts in a body of men numbering millions. However, all the information possible is collected and inquiries are made through every available channel—the Red Cross Society and other—and everything that can be done is done to lead to success.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
Help For Disablement.

Some of the best work the office has done has been to get pensions increased for /pen who have been permanently disabled and who had been inadequately provided for by the pension allowed. "

The* Soldiers' and Sailors' Help Society (of which Mr. Spinks is the local hon. secretary) assist discharged men by grants from the central fund.

Again, a father whose son had died of wounds in a distant hospital and wished to have him buried at home, but could not afford to pay for the journey, has had the necessary help to allow bim to gratify his heart's desire.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
MONEY AND ADVICE.

How The Resources Of The National

Fund Are Used.

In an—adjoining room is installed the office of the local branch of the Soldiers and Sailors Families' Association, under whose auspices nearly the whole of the money payments that go to the augmentation of. Government pay are paid out. The exceptions are payments to Territorial and National Reserve families, and grants made by firms to employes, which, of course, are taken into consideration when the amounts to be given are made up. This association is an old one and it has done much useful work in the past. Its own funds have long since been exhausted by, the demands of the wa,r, but its organisation has been absorbed by the Prince of Wales' National Fund, to which Warrington has liberally sub- scribed and from which Warrington dependents are now receiving material and welcome help. Here Mrs. George Dakin is in charge and she gets constant and devoted assistance from Mrs. Robert Garnett, Miss • Helen Fairclough, Mrs Seeker and Miss Melbourne.

Lady Greenall, the local president of the Association, was in active charge, in the early part of the war, and up to the time she went to France.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
Took Money With Both Hands.

' These ladies do more than pay out. money. They advise and help the recipients as much as they can. Sometimes their kindness is met by ingratitude, as in the case in which a woman had been lent an aggregate of £2O while wait- ing for /the War Office to get into, workifig order. When the woman received her '; back pay" from the War Office she sent a shilling of it on account of the money that had been advanced to her, and, for the time being, was expecting to make a profit of £l9 19s. out of the association's kindness. Steps were taken to cut short that pleasant dream. Now arrange- ments have beett made by which the advanced money is repaid direct to the association. In the main, however, the 'people recognise the great work the association is doing for them and are really' grateful. The Bond of Sympathy.

"Do you hear regularly from your son?T' Mrs. Dakin was asking,. as I entered the room, of a woman who evidently had a son in hospital abroad.

"X 8 about every three weeks."

"Then he is able'to write now?"

"Yes; he, has written this letter to my sister,". rand she produced. the latest news from her stricken son; the conversation demonstrat- ing, naturally and conclusively, the sympathy that exists between the helpers and the helped. As a matter of fact, very material help is given to families in case of sickness, though it is 144uented that rauch of. the trouble might be avoided if more thrifty methods were

===== 0002601_19150904_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
EXAMINER" MAN.

• adopted. Formerly, money was paid out -monthly, and, in some cases, was all spent at once, the first week's plenty being followed by a time of privation. Now the money is paid out weekly, and is spread out better over the requirements of the situation; but many colds are still caught by children for want of boots that, are not thought about until had weather comes to do the mischief before a remedy can be applied. • The association have the disposal of the Patriotic Fund. money locally. Immediately a woman loses her husband an application is made (miter behalf and she gets £5. and £1 for each child. They are given advice as to the best way of ilisposing of their mOney, but they are not always willing to take advice.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
TERRI,TORIALS AND NATIONAL

RESERVES.

The friends of the Territorials and National Reserve • have a room all to themselyes, in charge of Mr. Houghton and Mr. Cannel!, act- ing for the Borough Treasurer (Mr. John Fair- hurst) who has undertaken the care of the Territorials and the National Reserves. The money that is 'dealt out here on. the same lines as in the corresponding department for, the Regulars, is provided by the National Fund, through the Sailors' and Soldiers' Association, of which Mr. Fairhurst has become the local hon. secretary, in succession to Mr. Venables.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
Would You Like To See Your Husband?

When I reached this room it was evident the same spirit of sympathy and helpfulness pre- vailed as I had witnessed elsewhere.

I was just in time to see a wife whose hus- band was evidently in some far off hospital, receive her allowance.

"Do you want to go to see your husband?" asked Air. Cannell. The woman looked like someone who had,been confronted by some wondrous fairy tale. The obstacles in her way had made such a pleasure look impossible, and even now he did not care to plead the expense: "I cannot go" she said, "I have two young children." "Oh, fix the children up with your mother or some friend," was the helpful•suggestion,"and if you would like to go we will write for a pass for you."

The fairy tale had come true for he woman in surprisingly quick fashion, and one could see the priceless nature of the service that had been done her.

The service proffered as a matter of duty was left at that, and a kind inquiry as to the con- dition of the husband took the brief business- like conversation into another channel of human interest.

"He had his leg set but it was wrong, and they have had to break it again," said the wife sorrowfully but resignedly.

And we passed on to the next case, in which a young mother demonstrated that it is possible VI hold a baby in the left arm and write out a receipt with the other hand on paper resting upon a table about 2ft. 6in. high, at which she stood. She did this difficult feat quite well, and did not forget to say "Thank you" for the few shillings she received.

Some Of The Services Rendered.

• Allowances to the Territorials and National Reserves are given out at the Parr Hall on Wednesdays, but the Borough Treasurer's office is available for them throughout the week, for the seeking of advice upon any and every subject relating to the weltare of dependents. Over a thousand cases are being dealt with; and it is not surprising that the utmost con- fidence is reposed in the staff; on the other hand the staff have the highest opinion of all the women—with a very few exceptions indeed—who bring their little troubles to them. The same grants are made here as for the Regulars to tide dependents over till official allowances are placed formally on the Post Office list; and the Patriotic Fund grant to widows is procured. "We find many women like to invest these grants—some in the Savings Banks, some in the Co-operative Society—and one took a small amount in the War Loan," Mr. Houghton in- formed me.

"We make inquiries about wounded and missing Territorials," I was informed. "After the great Territorial charge on June 16th. several of our men were reported as missing. We made every inquiry about them in France, and we have received a letter from Boulogne to say that none.of, the men named were admitted to any hospital' in France between June 16th and July 12th.

"Another service we are able to do these people is to get them railway passes to visit the hospitals in which their wounded menfolk are lying. In dangerous cases the pass is free, and in slight cases half fare is allowed. We telegraphed for a pass for France, but the patient had taken a turn for the better and he was sent to England, his father seeing lira upon arrival"

===== 0002601_19150904_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
PRISONERS IN GERMANY.

How The Lot Of South Lancashire Men

Is Made Easier.

Perhaps the effort that is being made to make the lot of our prisoners in Germany as toler- able as possible is the most commendable of much, that is commendable in the work that is now being performed at. Pair Hall. Warrington confines its efforts in this matter to prisoners belonging to the South Lancashire Regiment, whose depot is here. Names are supplied by the Prisoners' Help Society, by friends and by prisoners themselves, who hear- ing of the effort that is being made to befriend them from their comrades in captivity, write to Mr. Spinks to be favoured, like others, with a few comforts.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
Will You Be A Stand-by ?

There are at the present time 400 South Las- cashire priSoners in Germany on the Warring- ton list, and to each one is sent a parcel containing 3s. worth of goods once a fortnight. The parcels are sent away in batches of 200 a week, 'so that each man receives a consignment on alternate weeks. The greatest grief of the staff and voluntary workers is that a parcel cannot be sent each week to each man, but that would cost £6O, and the fund, which is sup- ported by public subscriptions,eannot find more than £3O, a week at , present. Perhaps as the usefulness of this department •is recognised more financial assistance will be forthcoming. An effort, which 'promises to meet with success, is being made to induce individuals, institu- tions, churches, schools, friendly societies, trade unions, ,associations, and the like, to relieve the central office of the care of. -individual prisoners, and already "a stand-by has been found in respect of 60 prisoners, titled ladies, in distant parts of the country, in some cases having relieved Warrington of the care of men. The schools in Warrington have made a begin- ning, along the same lines, Mrs. Stansfield having induced her girls at Fairfield to pay for two prisoners; the staff of St. Peter's School have adopted two; Miss Place, Bolton Girls' School, two; Miss Finney, Heathside girls' and infants' department, has taken over one case; and Mr. Stansfield's boys at St. James have taken over another. It is hoped that this 'stand-by system, which gives the children a personal interest in the war, will spread.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
What The Parcels Contain.

When I visited Parr Hall on Wednesday afternoon I found tlie large room filled with long tables, which in happier times serve for tea parties, laid out with parcels ready for packing, tying and directing. 'For each of the 200 prisoners had been assigned a loaf, 11b. tin of syrup, }lb. tin Nestle's milk, lb. tea, sugar, 25 cigarettes, 2oz. tobacco, a tablet of soap (given by Crosfield's), six .oxo cubes, and 11b. oats. Next week the consignment will be slightly varied, hut this is a representative lot.

EVENING SCHOOLS.

Programme As Yet Unaffected By The War.

MEN AND WOMEN.

On Monday evening will re-open the War- rington Throning Schools—the Technical Insti- tute, the Commercial Institute, and the Branch Technical Schools at Bolton, Evelyn-street, and Fairfield (for women and girls), Beamont and Heathside (for men and youths). Syllabuses and further particulars may be obtained from the Director of Education. Aim of the Schools.

The Branch Technical Schools have been organised with a view to providing opportunities for boys and girls, who are not able to attend Secondary Schools, to have the education they have received in the public elementary schools continued, so that Viey may be prepared to pass on to the Commercial or Technical Institutes to receive their professional training. The Branch Technical Schools for boys specialise on the Commercial and Technical side, whilst those for girls specialise on the Coi,nmercial and Domestic side, and those who are successful in their studies will proceed to thq Commercial or Tech- nical Institute.

Special attention is drawn to the Industrial Course at the Beamont Branch Technical School, which is intended for boys working at trades who requirp to gain manual dexterity and at the same time improve their general, knowledge. Necessity of Qualification.

The Commercial and Technical Institutes have been organised so that the requirements of most students will be met. It is absolutely essential 'that ()very student should be properly qualified to take up the course of study. A. student who is not properly qualified in the preliminary subjects not, only wastes his own time but wastes that of his fellow students. Consequently no students will be admitted to the courses at the Commercial or Technical In- stitutes unless the Principals are' satisfied that they are qualified. An entrance examination to the Technical Institute will be held for all students desiring admission who cannot pro- duce evidence of qualification.

Students are required to take the full course of study, attend regularly, and take the neces- sary examinations. The Principals will exclude any student who does not fulfil these conditions. The students also will not be allowed to con- tinue in attendance unless they no the home- work; but any student finding that he has too much homework set should consult the Prin- cipal. It is a Matter of congratulation that despite the war the Education Committee have been able to go forward without alteration of their usual Programme. The arrangement of all the classes in the junior evening schools is practically the same as last year. Of course there have been difficulties, and there will almost certainly be 1 difficulties.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
Attendance In War-time.

Towards the end of last session it was found that the war somewhat affected many of the classes, particularly at the Technical Institute, though not unduly at the Commercial Institute (whose permanent prosperity would appear to be as firmly founded as that of the German Chan- cellor!) In all the higher courses it will be necessary for all those students who can to attend regularly, in order to maintain the standard.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
For Men And Women.

The usual strong bills of fare are offered at the schools for men and women. The schools for women will meet the requirements of those wishing to gain the instruction in dressmaking, cookery and millinery; and at the schools for men, elementary instruction in reading, writing and arithmetic will be given to men desirous of improving themselves.

It should he noted that only students 21 years of age and over will be admitted to these schools. The times of meeting set out in the syllabuses for these schools will be altered, if other times are found to be more convenient. There will be no examinations in connection with these classes.

These adult classes, which were only in- augurated last year, contain very great possi- bilities: and every Man or woman who can pos- sibly attend it is "up to"- to do so. It will be a very sad 'business for all concerned if these schools are allowed to suffer unduly through tht, war: and although—as can be seen—their programmes so far as can be seen will be carried out as before, any serious decrease in the num- ber of students might cause ro less than the abandonment of the classes. This would be a great pity, for once started the classes should not be " dropped " even. temporarily.

SCHOOL OF ART. Similar stipulations as to regular attendance, &c., govern the admittance of students to the evening classes at the School of Art: at which the following syllabus will obtain : __

:Montlay—Eiglit and shade; modelling orna- ment and the figurs; life claSs. Tuesday-- Drawing or ornament from Natilre and the antique; anatomy; practical design (cabinet makers', architectural, &xi). Wednesday— Light and shade; decorators'. practical work. Thursday—Object drawing; figure drawing; practical design and lettering; cabinet-work and carving. Friday—Drawing and painting; light and shade; geometrical drawing; metal- work; life class.—The embroidery and needle- work class will be arranged for Friday evenings if a sufficient number of entries is received at the coniniencement of the session.

In all schools the fees remain at the usual modest scale.

CROSFIELD'S BAND.

Two Fine Programmes Well Received At Chester.

Crosfield's famous band] gave two splendid concerts in the 'Chester Grove last week-end, in the presence of big and enthusiastically. appreciative audiences.

Tit-bits of Saturday's programme were an overture, "Egmont" (Beethoven); a cornet solo by Mr. J. Blackburn, "Old folks at home" (Round); and pieces from Rossini, Hume and Douglas. . On Sunday. two samples of Rimmer's work gave much pleasure, viz., " The Cossack" and his arrangement of the "Meyerbeer" selec- tion. 'A piece that was deservedly popular was "A military church parade" (Cope), and another . was the "Hallelujah" Chorus. Mr. J. Blackburn again played delightfully. , His selection was "Babylon" (S. Adams). Flow- tow's overture from "Martha" was a fine piece of playing. Amongst the encores, which were gratifyingly frequent, were "The Lost Chord" tSullivan) and "The Sailor's Grave."

By Wednesday night the parcels are ready for dispatch, and on Thursday morning the parcels are deposited atithe Post Office and started upon their mission. A separate advice note is sent to each man and a postcard is enclosed for an acknowledgment; and the replies that come in due course not only prove they have got into the right hands, but that the recipients are really thankful for the consideration that is being shown them by those at home. An un- delivered box has actually been returned by the Germans.

Births Down, Deaths Stationary.

In the week ended August 28th there were in Warrington 28 births (17 males and 11 females), against 44 (16 males and 28 females) in the cor- responding week last year. The deaths in- cluded two from scarlet fever and two from con- sumption. There were 24 new cases of scarlet fever, one of diphtheria and one of enteric.

Shorthafid Examination.

In connection with the recent Shorthand Ex- amination held at Wood's College, Warrington, the examiners have awarded prizes to the under- mentioned students of the College for meri- torious work: Miss Cook, best worked paper at 100 words per minute; Misses Malkin and Mar- tin, for excellent papers in the Advanced Theory stage; Miss Mortimer for Elementary Theory. The examination was held under the auspices of Pitman and Sons, Ltd.

Warrington Market Prices.

Wednesday. Wheat easier, with a likelihood of a further drop; 7s. 4d. to 7s. 6d. per 701 b. Oats (old), 4s. Bd. to 4s. 6d. per bushel of 451 b. New oats, of which there are good samples and very fair crops, were offered at 3s. 6d. -to 3s. 3d. per bushel of 451 b. Straw is better sold, 38s. to 40s. per ton being offered and given. Clover -hay (old), .26 10s. per ton; new hay, 10s. XX flour, 40s. per 2501 b. Best Bran, is. sd. per 201 b.

Should Harvest Festivals Be Held? Should harvest festivals be held this year? is a question that is sometimes heard. Surely yes. God's mercies to us are, and have been, very great, and we ought not to forget to thank Him for the kindly fruits of the earth. Our harvest festivals, then, will be hehl at the end of this month and at the beginning of October; the services will be of the same nature as usual. though the note of • jubilation which generally characterises such functions will be a little modified.—The Rev. Herbert B. Firth, in the "St. James' Parish Magazine."

Female Ascendancy. It was reported at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council on Wed,' nesday that the total number of births during the month in the Council's area was 15 (four. males and eleven females), the deaths numbered 20 (fourteen males and six females). The death- rate from all causes equalled 13.3 per thousand, as against an average of 8.83 for the past three months, and 19.0 for the correspond- ing Month of last year. The death-rate proper for the district, excluding persons who died in the district but did not belong to it, was 13.6.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
Treat To Workhouse Inmates.

On Monday, Mrs. Russell, of Newholme, Latchford, entertained 50 men from the infirm ward of the Warrington Workhouse to a drive in the country, followed by an excellent tea and games in her garden. On Tuesday she gave a similar delightful party to 50 women. She had many kind helpers, and the two afternoons were a great success, and gave immense pleasure to the inmates. Two blind men excited great aDplause by winning the musical chairs. All the company were photographed in a group by Mr. Price. On each occasion one of the guests made a graceful speech of thanks to Mr(. RusseN for her great kindness, and three hearty cheers were uiven,

. Fairground Fire. Shortly before midnight .on Saturday a he-- lated pedestrian observed that the tarpaulin cover of the Fairground roundabouts power engine was on fire. • A call was given to the Warrington Brigade, and Superintendent Turner and several of his men were on the spot in double quick time. The flames were promptly quenched, but the task was hardly accomplished before the fire-fighters received the tidings from a breathless informant that fire had broken out in Mr. Tyrer's pawnshoP, in Winwick-street.. Arriving with the motor engine at the shop in Winwick-street, the fire- men experienced some difficulty at the outset in ascertaining the whereabouts of the fire, but, once found, the flames were soon extin- guished. The damage was comparatively slight.

A Ramble In Bewsey. A country ramble took plate on Saturday afternoon in the neighbourhood of Bewsey, connection with the Junior Section of the Bew- sey Adult. School. Under the guidance of Mr. Hewitt, of Latchford, the boys .proceeded to search for various kinds of insects in fields and ponds. With the aid of a hand microscope brief studies of these lower forms of life were made. and useful knowledge was imparted to the juvenile minds. Wonder and amaiement were frequently expressed at the beauty and sym- metry of the forms found, and especially of the various ways by which Nature provided means of self-defence. After the ramble ten was generously provided in the school garden kr their leader and his wife, Mr. and Mrs. John IL Elwell. After tea several specimens collected in the afternoon were mounted on observation glasses and placed beneath a powerful micro- scope, and further studies made. Hearty thanks were accorded to Mr. Hewitt for his valuablo help and loan of microscopes.

'Warrington Lady In Railway Snlash• Miss Lillian Davey was in the ten &cluck Great Central express from Manchester to Lon- don which had a narrow escape last Friday. The axle of the third corridor coach broke at Bulwell Common, Nottingham, then the enuP" Hugs snapped, and the two rear coaches were hurled on to the bank of a cutting. The third coach also left the metals, and struck so vio- lently the brick support of a foot-bridge that the solid masonry was pushed backwards a foot and a portion thrown down. The irOn bridge was dislodged, but did not fall. MiB4 Davey, who was travelling to Aylesbury to EV) her brother, Mr. Reginald. Davey, a former Warrington journalist, was in this third coach, but fortunately she sustained nothing more serious than a shaking and a cut left ankle. Three passengers were injured and many sue": tamed bruises and shock. The up-line corkscrewed for 300 yards, and traffic had to be worked ca_:a single line for the rest tll9 1151.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 4, 1915.

ON HONOUR'S SCROLL.

the cause of his death, and two others were killed beside him.

"He is buried in a quiet spot beside others, in a quiet green spqt behind the trenches, where he fell .fighting for his country. A beautiful cross has been erected over his grave by one of his comrades, and I can assure you he was lowered into his last resting place by tender hands. I assure you of our deepest sym- pathy in your loss. May God be your strength and comfort in this dark hour.—Yours very sincerely, WILLIAM CRAWFORD (chaplain)." Captain R. M. Powell, Bth Seaforth High- landers, writing to Mrs. Southern, says:— "I can only tell you that I had the greatest opinion of him. He was quite one of my most promising young non-commissioned officers, and we all miss him very much inde:lld. I hope it may help You a little to hear your loss to feel that your son died doing his duty to the very best of his ability and showed a splendid ex- ample to his men. Please accept my sincerest sympathy. I only hope when the time comes we all do as well as your son." Present From Home Followed By Death.

It was on Wednesday, August 25th, that Lance-Corporal Southern was killed. That morning he had recetVed a new watch from his mother, and just before he was mortally wounded he *rote the following letter and sent his old watch home. The letter arrives the day before the news of his death and the old watch was delivered at the same time 'as the letter from‘Private Fraser.

"My Dear Mother,—l am writing you these few lines to let you know I have received the watch quite safe and I am sending you the other one back, as it is of no use to rile now that I have got the new one. The next parcel !you send me will you please send me a pair of socks, as the ones I have got are not much use for trench work, aka gets a little cold at night- time now; and a sponge would be very useful here, as we 'can't get any water to wski in. We have to use our drinking water. If you send a sponge, please send a rubber one, as they are the best for the trenches.

"Dear Mother, We had a little bad luck the other day. One of our men got his head blown off, so we are just beginning to think there is a war on; but don't upset yourself, as there is no ,one else hurt up to now, and another thing, I don't think this war will last much longer, as there has not been much doing these last two or three days, and I am hoping it is over before the winter sets in, as it is cold enough now, and I don't know what it will be like in another five or six weeks. I was sorry to hear about Tom Summerfield [his cousin] and his wife being drowned. It was a bit of bad luck for them. "Give my best love to all at home and tell my father I was asking about him and think- ing of him." Highly Respected At Orford.

Lance-Corporal W. Southern was 22 years of age. He celebrated his birthday in the trenches on August 15th. He received his education under Mr. John Cann ai Orford School and also attended the same church, and was highly thought of by the Vicar the Rev. G. Vale Owen) and all his schoolfellows. He' enlisted in the Bth Battalion Seaforth Highlanders on September Bth, 1914, and having been pro-

moted lance-corporal was drafted to France on

July 7th, 1915.- Prior to enlistment he was employed by the Warrington Corporation and worked at Longford Depot under Mr. R. who always spoke spry highly, of him and found him a good Workman.. After 'leaving school he worked for Mr. William Warburton, Dallam Farm, with whom he stayed for about five years, and who also spoke very highly of him.

KILLED.

C.-S.-M. WILLIAM BAKER.

Night-Watchman At Crosfield's.

Miss Smallman, of 1, Belmont-avenue, Latch- ford, has received news that Company-Sergeant- Major William Baker, of the 6th South Lanca- shire Regiment—and .who had lodged with her for several years—has been killed in action at the Dardanelles by a bullet wound in the head. Company-Sergeant-Major Baker was a widower, whose home address was with a relation at Liverpool, and it was from the latter place that the news was conveyed to Migs Smallman.

Company-Sergeant-Major Baker had retired

from the Army on a pension, but on the out- break of war he rejoined his old regiment. For. a time, before leaving for' the Front on Septem- ber 2nd, he was engaged in drilling recruits.. Before the war he was employed as a watchman at Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons. He was 46 years of age. Two of his nephews have been killed ih action, one having come from Australia in order to enlist.

LANCE-CORPL. SOUTHERN.

Killed After Writing A Cheer- Letter

Home:

Lance-corporal William Southern, of the Bth ,Seaforth Highlanders, son of Mr. William Southern, Ivy Cottage, Da'lam, near. Warring- ton, and brother of Mr. Thomas Southern, 63, Wilderspool-causeway, who is employed at Messrs.. Milling and Co.', has been killo in action.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
"A Nice Young Lad He Was."

Unofficial news of the casualty first came from Private R. Fraser, of the same battalion, Who, writing to friends in Warrington, said : - "We have had it very hot just this eight days. There are a few killed and wounded, and one of them that is killed is a lad from Warrington; they call him 'Bill' Southern. He comes from Longford; he lived just near Longford Hotel. God help his poor mother. lie is only a lad about twenty, and a nice young lad he was. I was a bit upset when I saw them getting knocked out, and wondering if I am going to pet it next. This young fellow reoeived a watch from home in the morning and was killed by shells' that night."

Private Ernest -Howard, of the same batta-

lion, who went to school ~at Orford with Lance- Corporal Southern and enlisted at the same time, also wrote respecting his " pal " Dear Mrs. Southern,—Justl a few lines to sympathise with you, in your great loss. No doubt you will have received a letter from the platoon .officer before you get this; It is very hard to realise yet that Willie is killed, as I was talking to .him a few minutes before he got hit., He died almost instantly, suffering no pain, as he was unconscious from being hit until he died. I have got his now watch and one or two other little things I will send in a day or two, as I have something to get from the Quartermaster-Sergeant yet. The other members of the 4latoon wish me to tender ,to you .their deepest sympathy at your sad. loss."

He was killed by a shell yesterday, and, luckily, instantaneously, so that the poor boy suffered no pain. He is a great loss to me and all of us, as I looked _ . _ _

upon him as one of my best N.C.O.'s, and he was universally loved."

Tribute From Battalion and Chaplain.

On Wednesday the following letters were received by Mrs. Southern:--

"I writ; on behalf of the officers, non-com- missioned officers and men of the Bth Battalion, Seaforth Highlanders, to express our very sin- cere sympathy with you on the loss of your gallant son, whb, as you have been informed, has been killed in action fighting for his King and country. "It will be arcomfort to you to know that his end was mercifully quick, and that he was spared from suffering and pain. -

"We laid him to rest in the little burying ground reserved for British soldiers near where he fell. The burial service was reverently read over his grave, which is marked by a small wooden cross erected by his comrades. We all sympathise with you most deeply.—l am, yours faithfully, N. A. THonmox, Lieut.-Col., and commanding Bth Seaforth Highlanders." "Yap will doubtless havb heard of the death of your son. He was killed almost instan- taneously in the trenches, on the night of the 25th August. He died a painless death, and in the doing of ,his duty. RIFLEMAN JAMES CROOKS.

Most Popular Man In Ills Company.

News has reached Mrs. Crooks, at Ellesmere- street, that her son, Rifleman James Crooks, of the King's Royal Rifles, has been killed in action. The news was conveyed in a letter, dated August 19th, from Lieut. R. S. T. Coch- rane, which ran as follows

" You will have heard, I suppose, by now, the sad news that your son, Rifleman J Crooks, was killed yesterday evening by the explosion in the trench of a German grenade, but I thought that I should like to send a line of my sincere sympathy to you in your loss. I can assure you that it was my loss also, for he was one of the most popular men in my platoon, and a great favourite with all his comrades. It will be a comfort for you to know that Ire could have suffered pain of no kind, and I feel sure that you are proud to think that he gave his life for his country, and met the most glorious end that can happen to anyone. I attended the funeral to-day in the beautiful soldiers' cemetery out here, and the grave, I hope, will be marked by a cross, bearing his name, in due time." Rifleman CrOoks, who was 34 years of age, was formerly employed as a box filer at the Dallam Forge. As a boy he attended St. Ann's School. He was very fond of football and billiards. An- ther brother, Isaac Crooks, is serving at the Front.

DIED OF WOUNDS.

RIFLEMAN F. KENT.

Bullet Wound Leads To Fatal Blood-

Poisoning.

Through the medium of a letter written by an Army chaplain, Mrs. RI Stephens received the news that her nephew, Rifleman Frank Kent, had been wounded in the thigh With a bullet. Later, news was sent to her that be had been admitted.. into the Victoria Hospital, Blackpool, while on Wednesday there came

RIFLE AN FRANK 1 NT. ' Died of Wounds.

word to say that as 'a result of the wound blood-poison had set in and that he had died of mortification. At the time that Rifleman Kent received the fatal wound he was acting as sniper. He took part in the battle of Neuve Chapelle and came through unscathed. The body was brought to Warrington on Friday and the funeral will take , place to- morrow (Sunday) afternoon. Rifleman Kent, 'who 'celebrated his twenty- first birthday on Monday, had lived with his uncle and aunt for a considerable time. As a boy, he attended the Silver-street Day School, and later was employed by Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, Ltd. He enlisted last September.

ST. BENEDICT'S ARCHITECT WOUNDED.

Captain Matthew lionan, of the Manchesters, has been wounded whilst fighting in the Dar- danelles. To Warringtonians he is well-known as the architect of the new St. Benedict's Church, Orford-lane.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE W. A. THOMAS.

Warrington-Canadian Dies In Germany.

Private William A. Thomas, of the 48th Highlanders (Canadian Regiment) who was re- ported missing last April, and was discovered to be a prisoner of war in the hands of the Germans at Luttick, suffering from gas poisoning, is now reported to have died, May 4th, from bronchitis. Private Thomas was the son of Colour. Sergeant John H. Thomas, formerly of Long- sham-street, Warrington, and prior to emigrat- ing to Canada he was employed at the Maypole Dairy Co.'s local branch. Private Thomas' father was in the 4th South Lancs. for 25 years. He emigrated to Toronto in 1913, and is now residing there. , Private Thomas joined the Canadian forces at the out- break of the war.

CAPTAIN HARMOOD-BANNER

KILLED.

Sir J. Harmood-Banner, M.P., ex-Lord Mayor of LiverpoM, and chairman of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., has received in- formation this week that his third son, Captain Walcot Harmood-Banner, was killed in action in France on August 30.

The deceased, who was 32 years Of age, joined the Ist Battalion South Wales Borderers in August last year, and was promoted ,captain in May. He has been at the Front since Novem- ber, being continually in action.

WOUNDED.

LANCE-CORPL. LEATHER NEXI TON

Wounded In The Arm.

Lance-Corporal Leather Newton, son of Mr. James Leather Newton, formerly of Bostock- street, and now of 8, Scott-street, Battersby- lane, has been wounded at the Dardanelles and is in hospital at Cairo.

Lance-Corporal Leather Newton, an old St. Barriabas' schoolboy, was eighteen last Febru- ary. He was employed at the Isolation Hos- pital, and joined the 6th South Lancashires at the outbreak of the war.

A letter received by his mother says Shot In The Right Forearm.

"I cannot use my right hand and a sister is kindly writing this for me. I am doing all right and you must .not worry about me. I got shot in the right forearm a week ago last Mon- day, and I Was brought here on the. following Friday. I shall probably be sent home before very _long on a hospital ship and it will be very nice to see you all again. It is not likely. that they will let me go back to the Front for a long time. I am quite well in health except for my wound, and we are very com-

SAPPER PARKINSON.

Wounded In Gallipoli While Assisting Infantry.

Sapper Robert Collin Parkinson, only son of Mr. James Parkinson, photographer, 79, Bridge-street, Warrington, has been wounded at the Dardanelles.

Sapper Parkinson is in the Royal Engineers. When war broke out he was on the staff of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, Ltd., Engineet, ing Department, Dallam. He is well-known in .athletic circles in Warrington and, district, being a member of the Warrington Cycle Club, Stockton Heath Hockey Club, Runcorn Boating Club and other kindred organisations.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
Water Worth Weight In Gold.

Writing from an Eastern hospital to his parents, Sapper Parkinson says:— " Our company landed on the Peninsula on , at a place of landing which had been forced the night previously. Our troops lost heavily that week-end. The Turks were evidently in big numbers and quite prepared.

"We sailed into the bay a little after dawn; a naval bombardment was in progress at the

SAPPER R C. PARKINSON, RE.

Wounded

time. It is marvellous to watch the big ships firing dead on the mark each time. Our first duty was to sink for water, which is worth its weight in gold on the Peninsula, but after sink- ing about 14ft. got drier still, so forced to give up the job. "I will ROW come to the time of my wound. There was,a, general advance, and I am pleased to say our fellows pushed the Turks back a considerable distance.

had to construct a trench from the ridge we were holding down towards the sea. We finiAed the job and returned to our bivouac; hadn't been down half an hour when orders came• to turn- out and put wire entangle- ments in front of the trench.

We Joined In The Fight.

"Things had been fairly quiet during the night, but about an hour before dawn it started again. The Turks counter-attacked, and by the time we got on the scene with our wire we were at it hammer and tongs, with our chaps practi- cally oa the ridge. It was impossible to put up

our wire, so we stuck down under cover, "All our officers. went forward somewhere, and we couldn't see our chaps getting it so hot without helping them, so we joirted in the fight. When I landed on the ridge top the Turks' trenches were only six or seven yards away.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
I Had Some Luck.

"We started banging at them. I fancy I had some luck, too. They then threw bombs at us. Oh, they are dreadful things! "I kept having to shift on acount of bombs, and eventually got into a place where I could look into their trench. I only 'fired about five rounds, when I got hit in my shoulder, which turned me clean over. Several of 'our chaps looked after me, and helped me down to the beach. Then we went on a hospital ship, and I was brought here. "Our first meal on the ship was only tread and butter and hot milk, but when one has had nothing but hard biscuits and bully beef for a considerable time, you can imagine how I appre- ciated it."

===== 0002601_19150904_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE J. CULLEN.

Wounded In The Attack On Hill 971.

Private Jas. Cullen, 6th South Lanes., son of Mr. Chas. Cullen, 13, Cyril-street, Warrington,

has been wounded in the leg at the Dardanelles, and at present is in hospital at Torquay. Writing tq his father and mother, with whom lib has left three children, being a widower, he says

"My leg is doing fine and I am walking about again. It was not much, so you must not worry. I must thank God I am living, for it was a cutting up our lot got taking 971 hill. We came from Acha Baba side and stopped in Shrapnel Gully for a test. That is where my Captain got wounded. They shelled us from morning till eleven o'clock at night. Then we started out for 971. It was in the knee the Captain got it, and I had to get him back to the beach hospital. Of course I landed him safely. I could not get a stretcher-bearer, and you may guess what I had on, being fully equipped: I could not leave my rifle, for there were snipers all around. We got along 'till we 'met the Leinsters and the Sergeant of the stretcher-bearers could see how I was fixed and, God bless him, he gave me two bearers and a stretcher. They sent him from the beach to the base. I don't know whether he knows I am home in England. lam sorry young Ernest James, my Canadian chum, has gone under, also my other officer.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
Officer's Last Words.

"I got to him and the last words he said were 'Have we got the hill?' To cheer him up I said ' 'Yes, sir.' He was an active little fellow, Mr. Willis by name. I could not tell you what happened for two days or more, but I am myself again and am back in England, so cheer up and tell the children their dada will see them soon."

Mr. Cullen has two other sons, Thomas and John, serving, the former in the Royal Field Artillery, who came from India at the out- break of the war, and the latter in the National Reserve.

urrt,E II ET HOME IN 'fIlE TRENCH.

BY J. WARD, ROYAL ENGINEERS.

I've a little wet home in a trench, Where the rain storms unceasingly drench From the sky overhead. Clay and mud is my

- \ bed, • - And t. t. stone that we used for a bench; Bully beef and hard biscuits we chew— It seems years since we tasted a stew; But though shells crack and scare, still mi place can compare With my little wet home, in the trench

0! the Huns in that trench o'er the way Seem to know that we're come here to stay; They shoot and they shout—but they can't get

us out, Though there's no dirty trick they won't play. For we don't like intruders, oh, no! —Which we told them straightforward. And, 10, • ,

Some departed quite sore, others stayed ever- more

Near my little wet home in the trench.

So hurrah for the mud and the clay!

And hurrah for Per Tag—that's "The Day" When we enter Berlin, that foul city of sin, And make the black-hearted .one pay.

. _ We won't mind all the cold, slush and stench, When from littns one just payment we

_ wrench: _ They will shed then, I fear, thicker stuff than

a tear— Through my little wet home in the trench

Sapper J. Ward, who senils from "somewhere in France" the above clever parody on the popular song, "Little Grey Home in the West," is an old soldier who accounts himself fortu- nate in having the privilege of helping the Allies to win the great war. In peace time Jiis home is at 9, Cyril-street, off Watkin-street, and he is employed by Messrs. Pearson and Knowles.

She Suspected That She Wasn't. "Are you a friend of the groom's family?" asked the usher at the church wedding. "I think not," replied the lady addressed; "I'm the mother of the bride."

Courage. That's the great, big thing that everyone can get out of life, no matter what's against him. Courage! We all need it—all the time. If we have it, life's worth all it costs and more. If we haven't got courage, life's a treadmill—a gallery—a chain-gang.--Clara g,. Laughlin,

THE TORPEDOED "ROYAL EDWARD."

LOCAL MEN "MISSING."

Official Report Fears That They

Were Drowned.

Two of the several local men who were on board the Royal Edward when she was torpedoed and sunk in the2Egean Sea, are officially reported to be missing, and it is feared that they have been drowned. These ere Privates William Hart, 3,. Cyril-street, and Thomas Kaye, 17, Watkin-street, who, as we stated in our last issue, were, companions, and both members of the South Wales Bqrderers. On Wednesday morning a message conveying the news of the feared fate air Private Hart was receiVed by his mother. Headed Infantry Record Office, Shrewalyary„ and signed by Captain G. H. Tennyson, the letter read as follows :—" I regret to have to inform you that a report has been received from the War Office to-day. that No. 19596, Private William Hart, ol the South Wales Borderers, is missing and it is feared that he has been drowned, consequent

PRIVATE W. HART. Missing.

upon the loss of the Royal Edward. Should any further information be received later it will at once be conveyed to you." A similar letter was received by the parents of Private Kaye. ' Two weeks ago, letters which had been posted at Malta, were received from each of the soldiers. _

Private Hart (aged 22), the \eldest son of Mrs. and the late Mr. William Hart, was formerly employed by Messrs. Peter Walker and Son, Ltd., and as a boy he attended St. Ann's School. He was a member of the " fLoyal Perseverance" Lodge of the National Independent Order of Oddfellows. He enlisted on April 24th. His father, who was for sixteen years in the South Lancashire Regiment, served throughout the Boer War, and was also in India for five

years. The youngest son of Mr. and Mrs. Thomas Kaye, Private Kaye was nineteen years of age when he- vacated his position as a moulder at Messrs. Richmond's, Grappenhall, three months ago, and enlisted. Before setting sail for the Dardanelles he came home on five days' leave. As a boy he attended the St. Benedict's School, and he worshipped at the Gospel Hall. His brother, Driver John Kaye, is serving at the Front with the R.F.A.

WARRINGTON STUDENT'S ESCAPE

Mrs. Doncaster, 24, St. Mary-street, War- rington, has received a letter from her grand- son, Private W. W. Dobson, son of Mr. Walter Dobson, who was formerly a cabinet-maker with Messrs. Robert Garnett and Sons, and

subsequently on his own account • in Butter- market-street, and is now in business in Liver- pool. Private Dobson, who was on the Royal Edward, is the nephew of Mr. Doncaster; hair- dresser, 6, 31 arket-street. He is a student in a Welsh Training College, and joined the Army along with a Amber of fellow-students. Private Dobson was in the water five or six hours before being rescued, but is now doing well and accounts himself exceptionally for- tunate.

RESCUED AFTER FIVE HOURS IN

THE WATER.

Private T. Penrose, of 33, Oxford-street, Leigh, and brother-in-law Of Mrs. Rigby, of Norris- street; Warrington, was one of the soldiers on board the ill-fated boat, the Royal Edward. He is a private in the South Wales Borderers. Writing from hospital .at Alexandria to his wife he says that he was in the water for five hours before being. rescued, along with several others.,

1,011 LIVES BELIEVED LOST. The Board of Trade on Wednesday night an- nounced the number and net tonnage of British wrecks during August and the number of lives lost. This shows:— Fifty Sailing vessels .(net tonnage 4.206), in- cluding 36 of 2,019 tons sunk by German war- ships, and 21 lives lost. Sixty-eight steamers (net tonnage 84,117), in- cluding 66 steamers of 81,490. tons sunk by German warships, and 1,178 lives. The lives lost include 1,011 which, so far as is known at present, were lost in the transport Royal Edward in the .tEgean Sea.

IVar Letters and Photographs.

We invite our readers to submit to us letters received from their soldier friends. We shall also be glad to receive photo- graphs of soldiers who have been killed, wounded, mentioned in dispatches or other- wise become a source of interest to the public. The letters will be carefully edited so that nothing will appear in print that ought not to appear. The photographs, when suitable, will be published in " The Examiner," which has an established reputation as a plcture paper. All letters and photographs are returned after use, undamaged, to the owners.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
STORIES FROM THE TRENCHES.

LITTERED WITH DEAD TURKS.

Lieutenant Miln Praises

Australians.

• Some interesting letters have been received by Mr. G. P. Miln, of Warrington and Chaster, from his son, Second-Lieutenant Gordon Miln, who is in Gallipoli with the Bth Battalion of the Cheshire Regiment. _ _ . . _ _

Writing Under the date of August 10th, he says •` As the papers at home will probably tell you, we are in the midst of a huge general engagement, which I devoutly trust will finish up the whole business here, but on the whole our regiment has had very good fortune so far, as nearly every other lot has suffered badly at one time or another. I had a party of 25 men under my charge on an out- lying hill for a couple of days anu was relieved yesterday. We did not have a casualty, but the relieving party had two men wounded just as they were taking over from me. The hill we occupied till yesterday was littgred with dead Turks. We are attached to the 'Australians at present, and I may tell you that they are a magnificent lot—their handiwork in the trenches is splendid. The Turks have an un- holy dread of them, and whereas a Turk, as a rule, seems to be very happy when taken prisoner by a British soldier, he always imagines he is in for a hot time if captured by an Australian."

===== 0002601_19150904_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
The Lack Of Cigarettes.

Writing again on August 15th, he says : " I am writing this about twenty yards be- yond the trenches, on the slope of a steep hill which commands a beautiful view of the country and sea. While I am writing now I can see the battleships bombarding the Turkish positions farther north, while we get bullets overhead from those of our enemy whose trenches are opposite ours. We were hard at work digging all last night in trenches, and came in at 4.30 for a sleep in our little dug-outs on the hillside." Commenting upon the Jack of cigarettes, Lieutenant Miln says—. "It is pathetic to see the joy on the men's faces if they should chance to have a few cigarettes sent them from home, and you should have seen the smile of my servant's face when I paid him his wages in a few cigarettes which I had managed to buy at about treble price at our base camp." He says letters are reaching Gallipoli in about 14 days after being posted in England, but there are serious delays in getting parcels sent by parcel ' post at high rites of postage. The Australian troops are much better served in this respect.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
"I LOST EVERYTHING"

P.S. "Terrier's" Experience 01 Great Charge.

At Sunday's Wycliffe P.S.A. meeting, the Secretary (Mr. B. Farrar) presided over & splendid attendance, and read the following letter from Private Arthur Payne, of the 4th South Lancashires: "You will no doubt be surprised to hear that I am with the boys again at the Front—sent up from Rouen. While I was there I thought I should have seen Mr. James. But no luck; he never came my way. There are many things I could tell you about, especially that memorable day of the charge. Other brothers that have gone home will no doubt be able to tell you something; but they don't always tell of the horrors that this war has brought about. There is one thing I lost that day, much to my regret. I prized it very much. That was the sleeping helmet that you so kindly sent me last Christ- mas .when we were stationed at Tunbridge Wells. Fancy carrying it all that time and then losing it! In fact, I lost everything that day, and now I am very thankful to be here to-day. I firmly believe there was more than one prayer answered. To see men go through a charge and then go on bended knees in prayer makes one feel that there is an end worth striving for. Give my kind regards to all the brethren. Best wishes to all."

===== 0002601_19150904_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
Four Hearty Welcomes.

The Chairman also heartily welcomed home two members—the Treasurer (Mr. IL Osborne) who had recovered from a severe operation, and Lance-Corporal nollinfon, again in their midst; and initiated two new members.

Mr. J. Himsworth then read in good style parts of an address given at St. Helens by the son of the Bishop of Liverpool, dealing with his experiences at the Front.

Special hymns which were sung dealt with God's guidance and protection. Mr. W. H. Payton supplied the organ accompaniment, and also gave an organ solo "Prelude," in his usual masterly style.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
RECRUITING.

Battalion Of Lancashire Volunteers To. Be Formed.

A meeting was held at the Town Hall on Tuesday afternoon to consider the formation of a Battalion of the Lancashire Volunteer Regi- ment for the 40th Recruiting Area. There were present: Alderman Peter Peacock (Mayor of Warrington, chairman), Sir David Gamble (Mayor of St. Helens), Councillor David Lewis (Mayor of Widnes), and representatives from the Warrington, Widnes and Prescot Training Corps, the Chairmen of the District Councils of Whiston, Prescot, Billlnge, Haydock, Newton- in-Makerfield and Warrington. The gentlemen present, together with the Chairmen of the Ashton-in-Makerfield and Gol- borne District Councils and the existing Command ants and future Commandants were appointed the Area Committee, and it was de :ded that steps be taken to form a Battalion under the name of the South-West •Lancashire Volunteer Regiment. The area was formed into three divisions, with centres respectively at St. Helens, Warrington and Widnes, and the Town Clerk of Warring- ton agreed to act temporarily as secretary to the Area Committee.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
WOUNDED SOLDIERS'

TREAT.

Visit To Chester.

The members of Crosfield's Band, on Satur- day, entertained a large party of wounded soldiers and nurses from Warrington at Eccleston Ferry; 'Chester, the necessary funds having been raised at a concert given in Bank Park by the band a few weeks ago. The party. left Warrington by the 1.30 p.m. train, and on arrival at Chester formed up, with the band leading, and marched to the river, where a couple of steamers, which had been chartered, were in waiting. The weather was fine, and the trip up the river was greatly enjoyed by. all. The band at intervals played lively airs, and Messrs. Brobbin and Hanley kindly gave duets on the concertinas. Tee. was served in an admirable manner shortly after arriving at Eccleston Ferry. The return journey was commenced .at six o'clock, and the party, with the exception of the band, that had an engagement in The Groves, Chester, for the evening, reached War- rington at 8.15. Everyone concerned thoroughly enjoyed the outing, and many were the thanks bestowed on the band for the able arrangements and the consideration shown to them during the trip.

Late Mr. A. Percival—A "Terrier" writes: "We were all surprised to hear about Alf. Percival [of Moston-street, whose death. we re- ported last week]. Poor chap, he went very suddenly." Send-off From Red Cross.—Yesterday morn- ing three soldiers, recovered from their wounds, departed from Brookfields—the Red Cross, Hospital—and from Lymm. At the station several of the nurses not on duty accorded them a most cordial send-oft.

Street Lighting in War-time.—The Council Gas Committee recommended that the ordin- ary street lamps be not lighted for the present season except clockwork lamps at the cross roads and dangerous corners—which should be lighted' as usual.—This the Council approved on Monday evening.

Captain Egerton-Warburion Dead.—Early this week, Captain Egerton-Warburton, of the Scots Guards, passed away in hospital from wounds received in Flanders many weeks ago. To Arley inhabitants, to whom he was an ideal squire, his loss will be a most severe one.

Volunteers at Chelford To-day.—By kind per- mission of Colonel George Dixon, regimental commandant of the Cheshire Volunteer Regi- ment, troops of five Ciheshire Battalions will give a military display this afternoon in Astle Park, Cholford. Amongst these will be the Lymm detachment of the Altrincham Battalion, who are to leave Lymm by train early in the afternoon.

Present At Council—At Monday evening's meeting of the Lymm Urban District Council there were present: Councillors R. H. Gotobed (in the chair), J. Hardy Holt, C. Cowper, S. Critchley, W. G. Groves, F. Lockyer, G. H. Parker and T. Warburton; and Messrs. W. Mullard (clerk), W. Timperley and Cecil Mort (assistant clerks), D. Mort (surveyor), C. 1. Barnes (water manager) and T. H. West (gas manager).

Cherry-lane Sermons.—The annual sermons in connection with the Zion Independent Methodist Church, Cherry-lane, were preached to very good congregations on Sunday by Mr. R. B. Woods, of Grappenhall. The preacher took for his morning text " Lord, I believe; help Thou mine unbelief"; whilst his evening discourse was based on "Surely the Lord is in the place, and I knew it not!" The day's collections proved very satisfactory.

Maltman's-road Sewer.—At a meeting of the Council Sanitary Committee letters regarding the proposed new sewer in Maltman's-road were read from Mr. Wm. Thomson and Mr. T. Wroe relative to the drainage in the road. The Clerk was directed to state that the matter is under consideration, and it was unanimously resolved that letters be written to the owners of 'property Abutting on the road, and to the trustees of Mr. Grundy, inviting them to meet the Sani- tary Committee and discuss the proposition.— The minute has been oonfirmed.

Tradesmen And Council At One.—At Monday's Council meeting Mr. Mullard read from the Lymm Tradesmen's Association the following letter: "At the quarterly meeting it was resolved that a letter of thanks, congratulation and support be sent to your Council re stand taken by them against the contemplated heavy increa3e of expenditure by the County Council, and they feel sure that the same economic 4 spirit will govern all expenses of their own.— Yours faithfully, Walter E. Hillier."—Cous- cillor Cowper: Very good!

Wesleyan Wedding.—At the Wesleyan Church on Wednesday afternoon the wedding was solemnised of Mr. Frank W. Perrin, youngest son of Mr. and Mrs, Peter Perrin, Sandy-lane, and Miss M. E. Hazlehurst, of Eagle-brow. The officiating clergyman was the Rev. A. J. Morton—who unfortunately was somewhat delayed by, his taxi breaking down on the jour- ney from Warrington. The chapel was beauti- fully decorated by the teachers and choir. The bride, who was given away by Mr. W. Pearson, son., was attired'in white,silk with a lace tunic; she wore a wreath and veil, and carried a lovely bouquet. The bridesmaids were Misses B. Hazlehurst and J. Pearson; they wore dresses of blue and green shot silk, and large black picture hats. Their bouquets were of sweet peas. Mr. John Mairs acted as best man, whilst the groomsmen were Messrs. W. Pearson and H. Perrin. Mr. H. S. Brown presided at the organ in able style. After the reception at the bridegroom's home—which was attended. amongst others, by the Sunday School Class of which Miss Hazlehurst was a teacher—Mr. and Mrs. Perrin left for the honeymoon. Mr. Perrin is the secretary to the Sunday School Clothing Club, whilst his wife acts as secretary for the Hockey Club. Both are choir members.

THE OLD MILL.

Council's Strong Remarks On "Very

Dangerjus " Property.

Towards the end of Monday's Council meeting Councillor Cowper directed attention to the fact that by the Old Mill Corner (in the village) a man had nearly been run into by a motor car, and also that a trap-door where the grain used to be put in was let down. "It is vary dangerous indeed," continued the Coun- cillor; "and the windows there anyone could get through. Mr. Clark (the agent) ought to do something so that it would not be so dangerous. That trap-door is always down. A man (Mr. Booth) has had his head cut very deep indeed! T. think we ought to ask Mr. Clark; he is greatly interested in the village, and I think it is only right. It is right' in the very heart of the village where it is—very dangerous in- deed!"

===== 0002601_19150904_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
"Any Child Might Lose Life."

• The Chairman (Councillor Gotobed) : I know the plactt. The door is absolutelY useless and might just as well be taken away. From the old window part of the framework is gone. and tiny child might clamber through. There is ft fearful drop of about forty feet [Several voices: Yes.] and any child might lose its life there. I saw Mr. Booth's, head. •

Councillor Cowper then proposed that 'Mr. Frank Clark be written to, drawing his atten4 ton to the state of the mill.

Councillor Groves seconded, and the motion was carried nem eon.

"GOOD BUSINESS."

Council's Fire Engine Igreement Will

Parii no ton-.

At Monday's Council meeting the Clerk read from the Partington Parish Council a letter accepting their offer of use of the fire engine ,at a retaining fee of £7 10s. per annum. Councillor Cowper: Well, that is good busi- ness!

Councillor Parker: It is very gratifying. If they had not done that I was going to propose that we do away with the engine. I didn't see why we should run a fire engine for the ,benefit of the insurance societies!

===== 0002601_19150904_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
MEDAL OF ST. GEORGE.

PRIVATE HENRY BROOKES ON - HOW HE GAINED IT.

Mrs. J. Taylor, of 1, West Hyde, received on Wednesday Torning the following letter from her son, Private Henry Brookes, of the 1/sth Cheshire Territorials. Last week we told how the gallant Private had 12een awarded Class 4 of the Russian Medal of St. George by the Czar :

"It came as a surprise to me when they told me that a wire had been received that I had got the honour. I don't feel that I have done any more than my duty. They have given it me for gallantry, as you will see, for attending to the wounded at Kemmel and Ypres. I feel rather proud, and no doubt you will be when you receive the news. I hope that you received ray letter telling you of Teddy Gilberts. This seems to be sending you good news on the top of the bad a few days ago.: • In a similar modest 'way Private Brookes writes to his wife at Heald Green, Cheadle.
