FREE FISHING FOR WOUNDED SOLDIERS.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—The Lymin Urban District Council have granted "the freedom of the lake" for fishing purposes to the wounded soldiers and attendants at the local Red Cross Hospital, but in order that this privilege may be enjoyed to the full, about a dozen or so fishing rods and lines are needed.

The men have already had one afternoon's sport with borrowed rods, and I can personally testify how much the brave fellows appreciated this innocent and restful relaxation.

My object in writing is to make an appeal, if I may be permitted, to those anglers of Lymm and district who may have rods and tackle which they have discarded, or for which they have no further use, to remember those who have "done their bit" for King and country. On behalf of the men I have promised to re- ceive any such gifts, and will gladly acknow- ledge their receipt to the donors. May I venture to ask for a speedy response, as the fine days are passing? Faithfully yours. R. HARRY GOTOBED.

The Cross, Lymm,, September 2fia, 1915

===== 0002601_19150904_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
FARNI-LABOj.'R SHORTAGE.

Councillor Cowper Befriends The Handicapped Farmers.

"A VITAL QUESTION."

At the Council meeting on Monday,

Councillor Cowper said: The farmers roun.d here are terribly short of labour. Are we doing all we can in this town to release men?

The Chairman (Councillor Gotobed): I take it we have had no applications, Mr. Mullard?

The Clerk: No.

Councillor Cowper: I have let my only. man go, and ho certainly has given his time away. He has done magnificently, and I think it is up .to everyone to do the same. Mr. D. Mort (surveyor): We have given three men to it. Two Were dismissed and .one left of his own accord.

The Chairman: It is certainly a vital ques- tion—the gathering of the harvest and the food supply. I think if we can release men we ought.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
Councillor Warburton's Suggestion.

Councillor Warburton: I think the right thing would be for it to be made known through the Press that our staff could be let off for three weeks or a month—if we can spare them. We would not discharge them, but let them off for the convenience of the farmer.

Possibility Of Child Labour.

The Chairman: Of course there is also this point: That a boy of the age of 13 can get away for three months by making application to the Education Authority. I don't know whether this is generally known. Mr. Mullard said the people did know of it, because the Education Committee had had applications. • Councillor Warburton then moved that the Surveyor help the farmers by letting off any man he could spare.

Councillor Holt seconded, and the approva was unanimous.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
PLAYGROUND DRAINAGE.

Council's Action Re Oughtrington School.

At Monday's Council meeting a letter was read from "Messrs. Beswick (of Chester, surveyors), showing -how they proposed to drain a part of the 'Oughtrington School playground where the surface water lay after heavy rains. Field drains, they added, would be laid and covered over with cinders.

The Council approved of the proposed play- ground drainage, the Chairman (Councillor Gotobed) remarking that they did not usually approve of 'surface water getting into their sewer*, but that this was an exceptional case, and the amount was so small it would not make much difterence. The matter has yet, of course, to be considered by' .the mr.nagers of the school.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
"PUBLIC ROADS" NOTICES.

Councillor Parker Raises An Importav Point.

Strong remarks were passed at Monday's Council meeting with reference to the removal of public notice boards. Councillor' Parker, who raised the question, stated that Mr. :Lockyer and himself went round with the Surveyor a few months ago and had erected finger-posts to mark public roads: That day he fond that one of them—near the golf links—had been ripped up and thrown into a ditch. "We didn't walk through the growing crops," continued Councillor Parker, "because of the hard times the farmers are going through; but now someone has pulled it up I think he ought to be found and summoned and made an example of. (Hear, hear.) I propose that Mr. Mort or some official be instructed to find out who did it."

The Chairman (Councillor Gotobed): It really doesn't do to fix the blame on the farmers. It might be one of the boys. Councillor Groves: But they should be prose- cuted whoever they are! The resolution was carried nem. con. May Walk Through Growing Crops.

Councillor Lockyer explained that they did not want to be hard upon the farmers, but he thought it was quite time the Council walked through all the footpaths and claimed a right of use on behalf of the public. Some of the farmers were trying to make an obstruction. The Chairman (to the speaker): You have no particular case to bring forward of road obstruction?

Councillor Lockyer : No, I have not. Councillor Parker pointed out that it was not obstruction when they ploughed it up, but if they put up thorns or bushes it would he. Councillor Lockyer : I know of one case where b.arbed-wire was put up! .Councillor Parker: If people plough up the footpaths, the public have a right to walk through the growing crops.

Miss Welford's Thanks.—" Hear, hear," was the Council's comment upon the following letter, read on Monday ,evening: "I received your letter telling me of my reappointment as governor of the Grammar School. Will you kindly convey my thanks to the members of the Council?—Yours very sincerely, Ethel M. Welford."

It'RIVATE WALTER HADDEN.

Wounded For Second Time In Wood Skirmish.

Mrs. Walter Hadden, of Park-view, Statham, received the news that her husband, Private Walter Hadden, of the 1/sth Cheshire Terri- torials, had been wounded, for the second time, in the following letter:— "I regret to inform, you that your husband, Walter, unfortunately is again wounded; but happily not of a serious nature. He was hit in his left ankle. He is being taken to the hospital; _there .he will receive every, attention. Will you kindly inform his mother, because she will worry a great deal if she hears un- officially? I hope he, will go home this time, for this is tile second time he has been'wounded and he deserves 'to go home. Along with the other men, he did excellent work. . . . It is a great blessing he was not killed.—l remain, faithfully yours, Sergeant IL CLARE." "Lucky To Be Living."

The night before, however, Mrs. Haddon had received from her brother-in-law, ,Lance-Corpl. Ernest Hadden (also of the " Terriers"), word which ran :

"Walter was wounded again yesterday, but I am again glad to say it is only a slight flesh- wound. . . . He and E. Gilberts did splen- didly until help came. . . I waited two hours at the Field Ambulance to try and see Walter; and at last they brought him—at twelve o'clock. He se,enAed quite all right, and told me he was very lucky to be living at all. He asked me to write to you. That was 12.30 last night, and the post goes in five minutes. I think he will get home this time."

===== 0002601_19150904_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
LYIUM TERRITORIALS.

) ADDITIONS TO ROLL OF HONOUR THIS WEEK.

The numerical strength of the Lymm " riers" at the Front has this week been reduced by four. For Private Edwin Gilberts has been killed, Private Walter Hadden wounded (for the second time), and two have transferred. Privates Percy and Joseph Clare have trans- ferred for a while from the sth Cheshires to the

3rd Section Royal Engineers. Writing .a week ago to Miss B. Cross, Sapper Percy Clare said: "We are still alive and kicking. Well, we were down in the time. We go to the trenches mining for eight hours, and come down for twenty-four hours. We go up again this afternoon." , It will be remembered that Private Henry Cadman made a similar transfer a few weeks ago.- .

===== 0002601_19150904_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
The Country's Call.

Private Percy Clare writes: "We cannot strike matches at night when we are on duty because the enemy would spot us. . . DM you 4lear about the big victory that-our men had, on sea the other day when they sunk ten or twelve boats of different kinds? We had it read out to us; it was on orders. You say does not like being on guard [in England] much. Then how would he like it here? He would be a bit nearer to the enemy where they come round at night on the same game as we are; but we like it a lot better than the trenches. Well, he has done well; he has not gone back on his word, and I admire him for sticking to it. But it would not do .for everyone to be the same. Where would •we have been to-day if the men had not come and heard their country's call? We have plenty more left in England; it is up to every young man to come and . . . help to put this lot to a finish. The sooner it is over, the better !"

PRIVATE EDWIN GILBERTS.

Killed Instantly In Wood By German

Patrol.

The news that Private Edwin Gilberts, of the Territorials, had been killed was first received by his parents at 16, Newfields, on Monday morning, in the following letter dated August 25th :

"It is with pain that I write this letter to inform you of your son Edwin's death, which happened at .9 a.m. this [Wednesday] morning. The Germans attacked the post he, along with other men, , was defending. • . The brave men promptly opened Ire on them and ' drove 'them off—which reflects great credit on them. The

PRIVATE E. GILBERTS.

Killed

Germans were seven to one in numbers; we scored a splendid victory. But poor,, Teddy's death has upset us all. Unfortunately, the Ger- mans whom our men wounded managed to get away. It was a very difficult wood for us to get through. Walter Hadden was wounded in his left leg—fortunately not of a serious nature. "It will no doubt console you to know Teddy died whilst defending the post he occupied for his King and country and his home against these cruel Germans. It is good for England we have such men.

"Our Lytia. m men _wish me to express to you their deep sympathy in this your sad bereave- ment. You have indeed lost a very dear and loving son. He has often talked to me of you; he was with you—as he was with us—loved and a great favourite. He never shirked duty no matter what it was, and at times that duty was very dangerous; he always did it with a good heart, knowing it was' all for the best. He will be buried by our Chaplain in the Soldiers' Ceme- tery; and I shall, along with your ,other brave son James, attend his funeral. The 'place will be communicated to you later. "I trust you will bear the loss bravely, and I feel sure the good people of Lymm will do all they can to comfort you in this great trial.— Yours faithfully, (Sergeant) H. CLARE."

Well-known " Goalie."

Aged twenty-five, Private W. Hadden is a native of Lymm—the son of Mrs. Hadden, of Pepper-street—and as a lad attended the Vil- lage School. Previous to the outbreak of war he was employed in the building of the new Co-operative Stores at The Cross. ===== 0002601_19150904_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
The Germans Had Fled!

Simultaneously there arrived from Private Henry Brookes a letter written at Private James Gilberts's request : "He was doing duty on a very dangerous post, which is known as the listening post. It is situated in a very thick wood, which lies about a quarter of a mile in front of the village we are holding. The wood is patrolled both by us and also by the Germans. On the morning of Wednesday a rustling noise was heard just in front, and immediately our patrol discerned this a bullet came and shot Teddy through the head; killing him instantly. Of course, a bit of a skirmish took place, in which Walter Hadden was wounded. We directly Went to theft' assist- ance, but the Germans had fled.

"We all sympathise with you in your great loss. . , . He died on a noble duty for the greatest and noblest cause

. • ; ."

Concealed Behind Bushes.

Private Henry Brookes writes to his mdther, Mrs. James. Taylor : `f He was . . . situated in A. wood, which is heavily planted with shrubs and trees. About nine' o'clock a rustling sound was heard a few yards in front of them, and they lay still wait- ing for their chance to fire. They guessed it was a patrol party of Germans who were con- cealed behind the bushes. Presently a party of eight or nine strappingly-built Germans emerged from the bushes into the open; whence a scrap took place between our party—first of three men against the Germans' eight or nine. One took a deliberate aim and shot Teddy through the head. By this time the Germans turned round to run away, but our fellows were simply pour- ing lead into them, as they were firing 'rapid.' "Later in the fray Walter Hadden got shot through the foot. We heard the firing, and immediately went to their assistance. But the Germans were flying in all directions. Some of them must have been hit badly, as there were blood tracks where they had run. They may pay us another visit, but still they are wel- come, for they will get . a much livelier time than they had—and that was hot for them !"

The Funeral Described.

Private James Gilberts, brother of the lett Territorial, wrote the following day "He was on duty with two more. The Ger- mans could have walked up to them and cap- tured them, but 'instead of that they fired. . . . How I shall live here and fight wain:nit him I do not know. . . . I should have liked to be with him till the last; T had been with him in every little thing, but this time I could not get. . . .. We have buried him peac,efully in a quiet village—in a churchyard in France. There was Harry Brookes and Fred Kay froth Broom- edge; Jeff Watson from Latchford; Jack Read from Rush Green; me and four more who Were strangers. We had a very good parson, and have put a nice cross on his grave." Private P. Clare says: "I feel sorry for Jim Gilberts; it has upset him. He is with me now." -

===== 0002601_19150904_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
" TERRIER " ON LEAVE.

Corporal Richard Percival Narrates His Impressions. Leave-turn, unexpectedly came round this week for another Lymm "Terrier" at the Front. The lucky person was Corporal Richard Perci- val, of 11, Moston-street, who was in one section of the firing line early on Tuesday night, when he was given two hours' notice to prepare for leave, and report at headquaeters. Within that time he was seated on, thefrivooden bench of a French train, speeding for a certain port, and. at eleven o'clock on Wednesday morning arrived at Lymm. ...Yesterday afternoon "The Examiner" man met him on Eagle Brow and had a long conver- sation. There will now be—the Corporal said— about sixteen Lymm Territorials left at the Front, and a similar number scattered up and down France and England, after transferring or being wounded and sick. He is not with them, as, of course, he was moved before leaving Eng- land to the charge of another section. All the chaps are quite well., though "fed up." However, the conditions abroad are im- proving greatly, and provisions of all sorts are plentiful. (This remark must not be con- strued into an excuse for Lymmites to stop send- ing parcels, each of which is always eagerly looked for and provides a fine party among the men!) They are also glad to be away from the trenches, and find it much easier—though still a "stiff job "—to guard the village and marsh. Of course they do not know how long they will remain there, but at least until the Corporal rejoins them. The village they find the most comfortable place they have stayed at since* going out. "We are living entirely like wild men—out in the woods. We sleep in them in winker-work bivouacs made by the French, who held the pesition, for nine months before us," said Corporal Percival." "The Weather has been fairly good, but we have had two or three very heavy thunderstorms, accompanied by rain, and in the pitch dark •nights. there has been lightning such as I never saw before. Happily the ground is very chalky, and so the rain dries quickly. "Captain Hartley is doing well like the rest of us. So is Sergeant Clare. Soon Come Out On Top. " It is my opinion that the war will not last much longer. In regard to all the seven months' fighting I have seen there has generally speak- ing, been little to choose between the standards of the two armies, but we are better equipped than the enemy. The reason we have not re- Pulsed them even better is that they do such dirty work, and we will not imitate it. Still, we are having the best of it now so far as I can see, and shall I think soon come out on top. "We have very little spare time out thgre, and what we do get we spend in sleep! Onlan average we get four to six hours' sleep in every twenty-four; but can just manage to 'carry on on this because we have much less marching than' formerly. "We don't see many Lymm men in other regiments—only when we happen to be. going in or coming out of the trenches. And then we just manage to pass a word with them. BUILDER AND OLD VOLUNTEER. Corporal Percival—who is in peace times Ei bricklayer under Mr. J. H. Holt, Booth's-hill— was an old ,:Volunteer, and has thus been with the local "Terriers" since their inauguration.' As lie says, he has seen .seven months' active service, during which he only missed sixteen days—which he spent in hospital with a slight complaint. Lymm, he is to leave on Wednes- day morning, and Will after reporting himself at London, return to the firing line on Wed- nesday night.

Private Stanley Gibbs, whthe hand is now nearly recovered, and who is looking particu- larly well, paid from Toxteth Park Military Hospital, Liverpool, a visit home this week. Arriving at Henry-street on Wednesday morn- ing of last week, he remained until Wednesday mid-day: anol hopes again to come home for this week-end. Afterwards he may rejoin the home. section of his regiment at Oswestry,

SERGEANT T. W. FIELD.

good health and spirits and am getting better of my wounds each hour. I had four shrapnel wounds off a Turks' shell; and one was in My ankle, one in my hip, one in my thumb and one in my ribs—all on the left side. But all except the one in my hip-bone are now healed up, and I' you this note sat up on my cot on the -high seas close to Malta. I am on the ss. Caledonia, a Glasgow ship, and she's a good one. It's full of wounded and we expect arriving at Southampton in about seven days after we pass Malta. We have, we believe, to pick somebody up at Malta, but I don't sup- pose that will delay us long. A Terrible Fight.

"I was in a charge in Turkey with. my boys, and the Turks met us and we had a terrible fight. But we got their trenches off them; and then they cut us cff, and I was three days and three nights fighting all the time until we were relieved. I am mentioned in dispatches for a V.C. Anyhow, I was put on a lighter at Gallipoli .and taken to Lemnos, and thence in the open sea the Caledonia picked us all off the small boat; and at the time of writing we are steaming full steam ahead for Malta, where I intend to hand this letter in.. So if I do come right through to Southampton I will send you a telegram the moment. the boat anchors in (lock.

"Don't worry, for I-am well looked after— nice and clean; and the voyage is doing me good. So cheer ho! and you will see I have been spared to come back to you with honour.." "Continued September lst.—l could not post the letter close to Malta, so I kept it in my pocket until we got to Gibraltar: We got the most dangerouq. cases off at 'Gib.,'. as they could not travel further; and then put out to sea. I was told at 'Gib.' that I would be home sooner than the letter, so I brought it on with me. I am here safe—and practically safe, as all my wounds have nearly healed up. I shall be home with you before your birthday, so cheer up! Fought Twelve Days Wounded In Four Places. "We have had a good voyage. On August Bth we had a terrific fight with the Turks= mind you, I have been fighting in Turkey for four solid months, but August the Bth put the lid on things. I left on August 20th. I fought twelve days wounded in four places, and then had to give in. So they put me on the Cale- donia and sent me home, where I landed safe. . . . As soon as I am discharged from hospital I will wire the train I am on, so that you can meet me. Now don't get excited! I told you I shiiuld be home for the fall of the leaf. . . . I am likely to move at any minute, as I am fit to travel!"

Writes to "The Examiner."

Yesterday in a letter to "The Examiner," sent from No. 2 Western (High-street) Military Hos- pital, Manchester; Sergeant Field says: "The Ss. Caledonia arrived in pevonport on Septem- ber Ist, with about 900 wiliunded and sick from the Dardankles. All regiments were repre- sented, including .Australian and Naval Re- serye,men. .1 was aljog.rd, ancU enjoyed, the voyage; you will notice it did not tald•long to get from DevonpOrt to Manchester. Although we arrived, late we had a royal welcome. My wounds are all healed except my left hip, which is doing well; we are .nice and cosy and are treated with warm affection from the hospital staff here."

Sergeant Field is well known to "Examiner" readers as a gifted correspondent. He belongs. of course, to the 1/7th Battalion Lancashire Fusiliers.

PRIVATE JAMES HOLT.

"I Lay For Three Hours—l Dare Not

Move ! "

Private James Holt, of the 6th South Lanca- shires, writes to his sister, Mrs. Richard Wib- berley, of Booth's Hill, from the Northern Hos- pital, Liverpool—where. be lies wounded. He says: "I don't think I shall be long before Pm out, but I don't know . what they will do with me yet. . . I got wounded , right through the muscle [of my left arm]. I think my arm will never be any more use to me unless , there's something done; but I'm hoping for the best. You know I got hit again in, the arm going to the boat, but it was only slight. I'm lucky to be here at all ; I believe all my com- rades are 'either killed or wounded: for such a sight I never saw in all my life. I lay for three hours—l dare not move, or I should have been killed, Thank .God I escaped !" Private Holt, whose age is 24, is a native of Lymm and ex-scholar of the village' school. After working as a toolmaker with Mr. Moss, a labourer on the canal, and other positions, he, went twelve months ago to lodgings in Warring- ton, working as a carter. .o'. He used to attend, the Lymm Parish Church, and is a single man. At Warrington he enlisted on the outbreak of war, and had seen two months' service at the Front. His parents were the late Mr. and Mrs. George Holt, of Booth's Hill.

DRINK IN THE TRENCHES.

Sir,—Mr. J. Travis, of Widnes, denounces the men who traffic in drink" as unscrupulous," governed by greed and selfishness," acc., &c., when they are simply engaged in a perfectly legal and necessary industry. Such foolish abuse is unfortunately too common among W- eaned temperance" advocates to merit more than passing notice, but a further remark of Mr. Travis' may deserve a moment's considera- tion. "Away in the trenches," he says, "the drink could be kept from the men; but at home it could not." If Mr. Travis had spent a few days in the trenches he would probably be very much delighted that the drink is not "kept away from the men,". and would realise ,the blessing that the rum ration has been to thousands of our soldiers. Thank Heaven, the military authorities are too much alive to the benefits of alcohol in war to be intimidated by teetotalist cranks !—Yours, &c., •

J. E. SIMPSON. Liverpool, August 30th, 1915.

SERGEANT GEORGE APPLIA'ON.

Widely-known Lymm Soldier Sniped At The Front. Sergeant George Appleton, of the 9th Battalion Rifle Brigade, has given his life at the Front. The news reached his parents at Cross-brow, Statham, on Monday night, in a comrade's letter, which ran:— " Saturday,—lt is with deepest regret that I have to inform you of your son George's death last Tuesday, somewhere between 6 and 8 p.m. It is very, very sad,-seeing that he had just previously been promoted to Sergeant for his gallant work, and had faced some of the .most terrible bombard- ments in this district, &ming out with- out a scratch in each instance, then should meet his death during such a quiet spell in ' the trenches: I expect you have heard him mention of Reg. Smith [a Manchester friend]. Well, he was wounded just before George—the two of them having gone to- gether right through. In fact we have all been together since the Camberley days, and expect you-will remember me when I tell you that George came to my home in Lon- don for a week-end whilst we were at Pet- worth. I would mention that poor old George was sniped by a German and that his death was instantaneous. "I have received a parcel and one or two newspapers, which I have opened; and in the case of the parcel have distributed the contents camong the boys, trusting that to be your wish. I have also received three letters, and will try and enclose them along with the note which I found in the parcel. "Must close now, asking you to accept my deepest sympathy in your sad bereave- ment.—l am. yours sincerely, Lance- Corporal A. H. PATTINSON." PRIMITIVE METHODIST WZRKER. Many circles in Lymm will miss the preknce of , Sergeant Appleton. Twenty-one years of age (he went across to France on the day he came of age), he was brought up at the Lymm School, and also at the Primitive Methodist Sunday School—later becoming secretary. "On behalf of the teachers and scholars," writes Mr. Ernest Moss to the bereaved family, "I wish tb tender our sincerest and deepest sympathy in your sad bereavement. We mourn the loss of your dear son exceedingly, for in him the schodl has lost one of its best, workers. He always had the welfare of the school at heart, and worked whole-heartedly for its greater proficiency. He has proved the true hero he was, in so much that the extra responsibilities have cost him his life. Our grief cannot be ex- pressed in words." He was a member of the choir, to which he had belonged from child- hood. "George was one of the best young men we have ever had about the church," writes Mr. J. Edwards, the choirmaster, "and in the choir he was above all praise—bright and cheery and spreading sunshine on all about him. Never to my tecollection was he respon- sible for a single word of complaint." Previous to enlisting immediately on the out- break-of war, the Sergeant was employed in the offices of Mr. G. L. Welford, solicitor, of Man- chester, where he had been for about two years. He was a former member of the Lads' Club, the Gymnasium, and the Saturday Football Club, from which most touching tributes have been received. Only two months ago he was wounded in the

SERGEANT C 4. APPLETON.

Killed.

shoulder and knee, after being an equal time at the Front, and had since seen several MOTO weeks' service—during which he gained his third promotion. As a thorough sportsman he at one time held the offices of captain and hon. treasurer of the Lymm Sunday Schools Athletic Club. In all spheres of life he leaves a wide circle of friends to mourn his loss, and much sympathy is felt with his bereaved parents, brothers and sisters. At a meeting of the Senior Christian En- deavour Society, held on Monday night, a sin- cere vote of condolence was passed.

ORFORD.

Vicar on Workers and Shirkers.—The annual Sunday school and choir sermons were preached in Orford Church on Sunday. The services were conducted by the Vicar (the Rev. G. Vale Owen). A flower "service, or rather a pound service or a gift service, was held in the after- noon, when everyone brought something— flowers, fruit, eggs, &c.—which were afterwards sent to the local hospitals. In the evening the, Vicar made an appeal to those doing any work for the Church to do it well, and to work as well for their Heavenly Master, as they were compelled to do for their earthly masters in order to receive their wages.. In every palish there- was a small inner circle Who never tired of their work, while others did as little as pos- sible, just when they thought fit. Anyone taking up work for the Church must see to it that it was work, and to be earnest and indus- trious. The collections were devoted to the . . Sunday school and choir funds.

PUBLIC POSTS.

Should They Be Advertised When Vacant?

A question involving the principle of whether vacancies for posts maintained by public money should be advertised in the usual way, with- -ont regard to private considerations, arose at the fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council on Wednesday afternoon, Mr. W. Furness presiding. The Clerk read a letter from the Local Gov- ernment Board suggesting that they had ex- Pected the Council to report before on the Matter of the appointment of a successor to their late medical officer, Dr. Sefrphton. _ _ _ _

In reply to a question, the Clerk said he did write to the Local Government Board and told them what the Council were doing in the matter.

"Something Behind It."

- The Chairman: They want you to do some- thing. Mr. Mather: A would-be candidate has been drawing the Local Government Board's atten- tion to what we did last meeting. • The Rev. A. M. Mitohell : I think` there has been something behind it. Mr. Knowles proposed that Dr. Sephton be permanently appointed by the Council that day. Ilie.ifitther gave them complete satisfaction, and the son had shown,hiinself very ce,pable at the work. He (Mr. Knowles) could see no reason for taking the position out of the family.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0192.txt | included=true | included =====
Give the Others a Chance.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell said he felt sure there must be several quite qualified medical men in the town who were ready to apply for the post, and he thought they should be given the oppor- tunity of doing so. He moved that the vacancy be advertised.

The Clerk: You would have to give notice. The ,Local Government Board are very strong on the point.

Mr. Mather Feels Sick.

Mr. Mather: Oh, never mind• about that. I'm sick. of • the Local Government Board. They're only men, and we're men here. I shall kick most strongly against it. I have had such a• lot of the Local Government Board in con- nection with the Guardians. •

Mr. Knowles: We can elect without notir. We can appoint without advertising, at .all. Mr. Ball seconded Mr. Knowles' resolution. The Fairest Way. .

The Chairman, in seconding,the Rev. A. M. Mitchell's amendment, said he did so because he thought that in an appointment like that, whoever was appointed should live in the dis- trict. By advertising they could give them- selves credit for having done Aleir utmost to obtain the very best man possible for the post. It was the fairest way of doing it. The profeg- Rion ought to have a fair chance of going in for the position. Mr. Mather: We passed a resolution at our Wit meeting that the temporary man was con- tinuing until further notice, and no notice has been given by any member of the Council to rescind the resolution. He, personally, did not feel inclined to'vote on the matter, unless other means were adopted.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0193.txt | included=true | included =====
The Dauntless Three.

The Clerk said that no decision made •in the matter woula he of any standing unless at least half the Council voted for it. On the question being put to the rote, there held up their hands for the amendment two Members, and for the original proposition three. It was decided to communicate -the Council'p action to the Local .Government Board.

CH ILD WELFARE.

An important Scheme To Be Suspended Till After The War.

Dr. Sephton reported at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council that the Notification Of Births Act 1907 had been extended, and now became compulsory in every district from that day (September Ist). He had been in communication with several medi- cal officers of health in several districts, and the general feeling seemed to be that, as far as 'possible, Councils should carry out the Act B'B to notifications; but, until some -scheme was formulated, any ideas of maternity clinics, calls bY health visitors or the removal into hospitals or maternity homes of cases of people living in unsuitable' dwellings, should, until the end Of the war, be left in abeyance. A letter from Mr. Jas. E. Gee, on behalf of r. Sergeant, Lancashire County Medical Officer of Health, in reference to the Act was also read by Dr. Sephton. At the last meeting of. the County Public Health Committee the County Medical Officer of Health was instructed to prepare a Complete child welfare scheme, but the feeling of the committee was that such a scheme sh.guld not be put into operation at the present time owing to the war. The county scheme would probably embrace the whole connty, with the exception of non- -connty boroughs and the urban districts with Populations of over 20,000, and the committee did not wish to discotrage local efforts as regarded child welfare work.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT GERMANY WANTS.

Demands For Wholesale Annexation On Both Frontiers.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
FRENCH AND BELGIAN IRON AND COAL; POLISH AGRICULTURAL LAND NEEDED.

The. chief agrarian and industrial organisa- tions of Germany recently presented to the German Imperial Chancellor a confidential memorandum setting forth the enemy's desires and aims respecting the land... they have con- quered or hope to conquer. The rtemorandum, whii h bean; the da:e of May 20th, is signed by ,•eprdentatives of the League of Agrarians, the League ~.)f -Gt.rmar peasants, the controlling group of the Christian Associations of German peasants (known as the Westphalian grollP)”:rthe entral Union of German Manufacturers, the League of Manu- facturers, and the Union of the Middle Classes of the Empire. The document is addressed to the Imperial Chancellor, but copies, it appears from the text, were also sent to the other Minis-. ters and to the„various Confederate States. The memorandum .begins by. expressing the resolution of the six organisations to maintain the struggle, no matter what' the sacrifices, in order that the country may emerge victorious with added power abroad and the guarantee of durable peace. It recalls in this respect with satisfaction the declaration of the " Nord- duetsche Aligemeine Zeitung" denying 'rumours of peace proposals to England. "No premature peace," it says. "And no indecisive peace either. No peace which does not take full advantage in the political realm of the final success which we hope, to gain in the military idomain. Certainly our country would bow betrire the necessities. of a military situation which set up insurmountable barriers against the, most enthusiastic will to hold our gains. But if at the conclusttm of peace any sign was given, without military necessit?6, of inopportune weakness which, besides-entailing a premature abandonment of the struggle, would also mean an ovet-whelming burden of taxation for our warriors—such weakness would in- evitably have the most dangerous consequences for the inner peace of our country." The manifesto then promeds to set ~forth in detail the conditions which the six.prganisations consider necessary for "an honourable peace corresponding to the sacrifices incurred and containing in it.the guarantee of .its durability." - We read this respect it must never be forgotten that our enemies are 'constantly de- claring that Germany must be .crushed and blotted out of the list of great Powers. In the face of such' asvirations we can find no protec- tion in. treaties which will be trodden under- foot at the opportune moment; our only guaran- tee consists in such a military and economic weakening of our adversaries that peace in con- sequence will be guaranteed for a determined period"

monthly output of steel is more than a million tons. Ail(' yet there is no superfluity of iron or steel, rather a deficit. "The manufacture of shells demands incred- ible quantities of iron and steel. For shells of grey metal alone, the inferior substitutes for rifled shells and shells of east steel, at least 4,000 tons per day of pig iron have been used during the_last few months. No precise figures are available at tile.. moment. But one thing is certain. If the production of cast iron and steel had not been doubljed since' last August it would have been impossible to continue the. war. Oolithic iron ore, the primary element for the manufacture of iron and steel, is assuming increasing importance, for only this mineral is being found in Germany in increas- ing quantity. The production of the other' home areas is extremely limited, and • importation from overseas, even from Sweden, is rendered so difficult that in many districts, even apart from Luxembourg and Lorraine, oolithic iron represents 60 per cent. or 60 per cent. of the production of pig iron or steel. "It should be noticed at the same time that the high proportion of steel extracted from oolithic iron ore offers the only possibility of supplying German agriculture with the neces- sary phosphoric acid when the importance of prosphates is stopped. Therefore the security of - the German Empire in a' 'future. war' de-. mands the. possession of all the deposits of oolithic iron ore,- including the fortresses of Longwy- and Verdun;, Without which these de- posits could not be defended."

WHY COALFIELDS MUST BE TAKEN. The possession of Large quantities of coal, and especially of Hell bituminous coal which abounds in the northern districts of France, !s a determining factor in the issue of the war in at least as great a measure as iron ore. Bel- gium and North France together produce more gian 40,000,000 toils., " From to-day, a.s is shown by the embargo on coal by England, this material is One of the decisive factors of political influence. lAdus- trial neutral States are forced to make them- selves tire instruments of that belligertnt party which can assure them their sxpply of coal. At present we are not in a position to 'do this to the ,extent required, and from to-day we are forced to have recourse to the production of Belgium, so as not to let our neutral neigh- bours Income completely dependent on Eng- land. It is extremely probable that the syste- matic intensification of Belgian production will after the war have the utmost importance for the maintenance of the neutrality of various neutral States.

"The fact that coal, which gives coke or gas, contains at the same time the essential elements of our principal explosives, is dtiubtless known to all, just as is the importance of coal for the manufacture of ammonia. Further, coal gives us, in benzol, the sole substitute for ben- zine which we lack; and, finally, coal-tar fur- nishes us with the oil for heating purposes necessary to the navy, as well as with the most satisfactory lubricant, and with what is per- haps the chief element in artificial petrol. "In this respect let us recall the fact that it seems impossible to perfect the torpedo-boat or submarine without abundant liquid com- bustibles. This wax. has proved the superiority 'of oil fuel over the exclusive use of coal-fuel for torpedo-boats with such overwhelming -evidence that it would be unpardonably frivolous not to draw al? the consequences from it for the future.

" If our hostile neighbours secure the sources of mineral oil, Germany must take care to secure the necessary supplies of coal, and to transform them in times of peace into inex- haustible sources of oil, benzol, toluol, ammonia, and naphthaline, not only to in- crease prosperity in times of peace but for the indispensable preparation for war."

===== 0002601_19150904_art0199.txt | included=true | included =====
, THE DEMANDS.

Besides the reconquest of a colonial empire amply satisfying the multiple economic in- terests of Germany, besides guarantees for the future of its commercial and fiscal policy and the obtaining of a satisfactory war indemnity paid in an appropriate form, the following demands are formulated

Given the necessity of assuring our sea- pOwer,,given our future economic and military futuref in relation to England, and the close Connections, economically so important, between Belgium and our chief industrial territories, Belgium must be, from the military and fiscal standpoint as well as from the financial and postal, subordinated to the legislation of the German Empire. Its railways, canals and waterways must be attached to our systeni. of transport and communications. While con- stituting two separate provinces—one Walloon, the other Flemish,—while taking into German hands the economic enterprises and properties necessary for the domination of the country, the country must be governed and administered in such a way that its inhabitants will acquire no influence over the political destinies of the German Empire. "As for France, considering our position with regard to England, the possession of the littoral from the Belgian frontier approximately down to the Somme, and of the outlet to the Atlantic, must be regarded as a vital question for our future power on the sea. The hinterland to be acquired at the same time must be of such an extent that it will be possible to take full ad- vantage, both from Ulf economical and strategical point of view, of the ports obtained in the Channel. Any other territorial acquisition in France, apart from the necessary annexa- tion of the mining basins of Briey, must only be made in consideration of military strategy. "In this respect it may be regarded as a matter of course, after the experiences of this war, that we shOuld not expose our frontiers to hostile invasion by leaving our enemies the fortified positions which threaten us, especially Vertrun and Belfort, and the western slope of the Vosges which lies between those two towns. By the acouisition of the line of the Meuse and the Trench coast of the Channel we should possess, besides the mining basin of Briey, the coal basins of the departments of the North and the Pas de Calias.

" Tho treatment extended to the inhabitants of the annexed districts would have to be" adds the manifesto, "after the experiences in Alsace- Lorraine, the same as that proposed for Belgium."

===== 0002601_19150904_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
ACQUISITIONS IN THE EAST.

In the east also Germany is to take with a free hand. As for the east, the primary con- sideration is as follows : • •

" Th:! great increase in industrial power in the west _must be counterbalanced by the acquisition of equivalent agricultural territory. The present economic structure of Germany has shown itself so favourable during the present war that the necessity of maintaining it in the :11.1tIPT iny b 9midered as the general con- ._ viction of our pe,opie, "Furth; tlttieeeity of strengthening the sound tip .tultur al bases of Our national' ecolibbly, of rendering possible German agrit-. eititiiial colonisation on a large scale, as well as the repatriation within the Empire of the German peasants living abroad, notably in Russia, to-day deprived of all rights; finally, the necessity of increasing the contingents liable to military service—all this demands a consider- able extension of the frontiers of the Empire and of Prussia towards the east, but the annexa- tion', or at least partial annexation, of the Baltic provinces and the territories which bound th.cm on the south. Nor must it be forgotten that our frontier in East Prussia must be safeguarded from the military point of view. "As for West Prussia, Posen and Silesia, they must not remain frontier provinces exposed as they are to-day. The war indemnity to be paid by RUsSia must for the larger 'part consist of territorial cessions. As for the political rights to be given the inhabitants, we refer to what we said in the case of France."

===== 0002601_19150904_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT IS TO BE SEIZED, AND WHY.

These are the main outlines of the demands of the associations; but there follow a number of interesting passages dealing with the re- sources of the districts which it is proposed to annex, and their necessity to Germany in her preparation for future wars. "if German agriculture had not been suffi- cient to ensure the nourishment of our people," says the memorandum, " in spite of all efforts on the part of our enemies, and if German in- dustry, German invention, and Geiman engin- eering had not been in a position to make us independent of the foreigner in the most diverse domains, we should have had to succumb .finally in the struggle which has been imposed upon us, even if we had not succumbed at the ,begin- ning. "This is in our minds when we demand on the one hand the annexation of territories for agricultural- colonisation, and on the other hand the mining area of Meurthe-et-Moselle, as well as the French coal areas in the depart- ments of the North and the Pas-de-Calais, be- sides the Belgian areas. "Annexations like those of the mining and coal areas we have mentioned would not only favour the extension of oiiir industrial power, but they constitute a military neeessitk., as may be seen from the following considerations. " The monthly output of pig iron in Germany has risen since August, 1914, to a million tons —that is to say, it has almost doubled. The

===== 0002601_19150904_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
NOT A "POLICY OF CONQUEST."

The conclusion of the whole thing is as follows :

"The preceding demands, it need not be said, have for condition the possibility of our army realising them. But considering the results already obtained, we are confident that our chiefs and army will -obtain a victory which will, assure us our ends. It is not out of a policy of conquest that we must try and. attain these ends, but because their realisation alone will guarantee a durable peace, the peace which the whole German people awaits after the great sacrifices it has united to make." .

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 4, RIFLE SHOOTING.

Forthcoming Open Prize Meeting At Appleton.

In. order to encourage rifle, shooting in ..the neighbourhood, the Appleton Rifle (Nub have decid4l to hold a prize meeting at their range on Saturday, September 18th, the meeting to be open to Warrington and district. 'Seven competitions have been arranged, including a disc contest- between teams from the various rifle clubs in the neighbourhood, and also a competition open to members of the local Volunteer Training Corps, to be shot with open sights. The meeting should be an interesting one, and, - if successful, will probably pave thc way to a more enterprising event next year. The arrangements are in the hands of a sub- committee consisting of Messrs. A. Shepherd., A Towers and R. M. Swinton, who will be pleased to give any information required. Full details will be issued as soon as possible.

ECONOMY AND THE DRINK TRADE.

Mr. S. A. Gamble writes to the " Mari Chester Guardian ":—Being connected with a philan- thropic institution in Manchester, I have re ceived a circular from the "Board of Trade urging The importance of economy in meat, and another advising the use of granulated and crystal sugar instead of loaf, also on the ground of economy. Very good advice, and we shall try to follow it; but what about the tons of sugar and grain wasted in the manufacture of beer? The public-houses in this city open from 10.30 in the morning till 10 at night, and in certain districts at every street corner and- between ',the corners. Postal economy is now proposed, and business men say the restrictions would injure them. Why don't•they rise against the trade that is injuring diem most, and taking £500,000 a day, much of which would otherwise be spent with them? Bishop Gore asks what is the subtle influence, wllich saddles us with the drink tade. There is but one answer. The money made by those engaged in it, regardless of what it costs the nation, in the .deterioration of the-people; body; son Land-spirit many tyears must the war last before we learn this lesson? •

Why There Wilt Be No Time For Sport.

Cricket —Dreista.benschlagerspiel. Leg before'-13eiRenschwindel. • Not out —Nochnichtabgema.cht. Wicket— Dreistabeneinrichtung. Half-time— Halbspielwartepause. A FRIENDLY GAME.

LIBERAL CLUB v. COMMERCIAL TRAVELLERS.

On the green. of the Liberal Club qn Satur- day--afternoon an interesting bowling match took place between the Liberal Bowling Club and the Warrington Commercial Travellers' Association. The green, thanks to the earnest attention given to it by the new steward, Mr. Whitfield, was in splendid condition, and a good game resulted in a win for the Liberal Club by 80 points on the single games and 37 on the double games. The scores were as follows:

TEAMS DRINK TEA AS FRIENDS.

The match over, both teams retired to the club for tea, which had been splendidly served by Mrs. Whitfield. In addition to Alderman D. Tinnion, who presided, there were present at the tea table, amongst others, Mr. Walter P. Ashton (chairman of the Travellers' Associa- tion); and. the President of the :Widnes .Liberal Club.

On behalf of the Liberal Club, Alderman Tinnion welcomed the Travellers, and went on to 'speak id the, bad conditiOn of trade as far as they '(the Travellers) were concerned, lie then called upon all present to drink to the health of the King. • Councillor Wilkinson supported, and referred to the pleasant time they had had that- after- noon.

Mr. Ashton, in returning thanks for the wel- come bestowed upon. the Travellers, expressed the hope that the next. time they met on the green the 'Travellers would do much better than they had done that afternoon.. '

Mr. W. G. Hayes, on behalf of the Travellers, proposed a hearty vote of thanks to Alderman Tinnion foi presiding. Mr. Fred Howard, in seconding. referred to the courtesy with which they had been received by the Liberal Bowling Club. Votes of thanks were also proposed to the steward and stewardess (Mr. and Mrs. Whit- field) for their excellent catering, to Mr. Wm. Owen (secretary of the Travellers' Association), and to Mr. George Marshall (secretary of the Liberal Bowling Club) for so ably carrying out the arrangements in connection with the func- tion. •

SECOND WAR FUND IL4NDICAP.

A second War Fund'handicap will be .played to-day, commencing at three o'clock. The em- tries will not close for a few weeks, and it is to hoped that it will prove as successful as did the one recently held. The committee are deter- mined that only amateurs shall take part in any of their competitions. Several who have broken the rules have been struck out of all competitions, and will not be allowed to take part in .any amateur competitions in the town until they are reinstated by the British Crown Green Amateur Bowling Association. THE WAR.,BUDGFF.

From so uniformly excellent a number as the. issue of "The War Budget," out Thursday, September 2nd, it is difficult to select any one article or piPture as the outstanding "feature." To, mention the pictorial attractiveness first, theie , are over seventy pictures—photographs from 'the various fighting fronts, and more of the vivid drawings by popular war artists which are so characteristic of this journal. The cover, by the way, is a new departure for "The War Budget," the first of its kind appeared last week. 1 Amongst the articles—every one of them on a sUbjeet of paramount appeal—we may mention the fifth of the " Munitions" series, this week's telling of the great work being done at Birming- ham; the weekly review and forecast by James C. Backhouse; an article on how the 'Navy is fed; and the weekly department devoted to the Voluntary Training Corps,

AT WILDERSPOOL.

Junior Matches To Start Next

Saturday.

SCHEME TO COVER EXPENSES.

The following circular has been issued to the 1, friends and supporters of the Warrington Football Club :—_

"As you are doubtless aware, the committee have„deoided unanimously not to:take part in Senior Football for the duration of the :war, and feel sure that they have the wholehearted support of the members in their .action. "They have, however, decided to encourage Junior Football (the age limit being fixed at 18), and fixtures in connection with the War- rington Schoolboys' League and Intermediate League are being completed. Matches-will be played each Saturday on the Wilderspool Ground, commencing the 11th inst. "The financial position of the club has been seriously considered, and, taking all the cir- cumstances into consideration, the committee have decided to meet the members by reducing the subscription for the current season to Honorary members, 7e. 6d.; ordinary members, 35.; stand, 2s. extra:. "The committee appeal to the loyalty of the members to enable them to meet all current charges so- as to be in a position, when the war is over, to restart without any financial obliga- tions.

".Yours truly, on behalf of the committee, EDMUND OSBORNE; President; T. F. BARBEL Chairman; T. PEMBERTON, Hon. Treasurer; T. P. BRADSHAW, Hon. Secretary."

INTERMEDiATE LEAGUE.

At a meeting during the week. the last date on which applications for membership of the League will be received was fixed for Septembex 11th.

Clubs may enter at once, and will receive registration forms as soon as the membership fee is received.

Will all those who intend to join the Le-agne please do so at once? Whitecross Wire Ropery have been granted permission to join- Will others please follow? All players must be under 18 on the 30th April, 1916. In the case of works' teams, their players must not be old boys of schools. repre- sented in the League.

It is ,to be hoped that those who played last year and are ineligible this year will do their best to help to organise their teams, although they cannot play themselves. Application for membership can be made to Mr. E. Jackson Mee, hon. sec., Mersey View, Penketh: or Mr. A. Malcolm, hon. treasurer, 113, Wilderspool-road.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL AMUSEMENTS.

WAR-PLAY STAGED AT THE ROYAL COURT'.

The-,ever-growing British Amy has for over a year been affirming with stentorian ,harmony that the distance to Tipperary is something quite considerable, so that we who, not being in khaki, are not in the know on that very im- pdtant point have felt that we needs must believe. Still, .we have never felt quite con- vinced, and it comes as a harbinger of increased stability for our unsettled minds that some discerning student of psychology has seen fit to convert the words of the never-to-be-forgotten ditty to the title of a play and so allay a great deal of, if not all, the doubt that had long been so voracious a canker as to prove greatly detri- mental to the tranquility that is characteilttic of the Briton. .

The play that is being presented this week at the Royal Court Theatre, with the first line of " Tommy's " marching song as its title, has &roused a good deal of enthusiasm. As the title indicates, the production is a war-play. It is. perhaps, hardly necessary to say that the villain is a spy; it is certainly not requisite for us to mention that he is a, German. On that point originality would hardly be tolerated, or if tolerated, certainly not sufficiently appreci- aqd to make the experiment in novelty worth the candle. He is a monster, this German, as, of course, one would expect to find him; the character, like others, is so drawn as to render it not too morbidly. life-like. A healthier aspect is the portrayal of the characterigtic heart and spirit of the British soldiers. Scenes in which this beloved figure covers himself with -glory are naturally cheered to the echo. And there are stirring incident!

The hero of the piece is a young Briton who is transformed by the war. His name is Hugh Chancellor, and he is "done proud" by Mr. Walter Clarke. 'ratty Mayne, the heroine, from "over the pond," is thoroughly por- trayed by Miss Joan Ellis. Mr. Arthur L. Read does the German Spy to perfection. Mr. Dallas H. Keand, Miss Dorothy Louise and Miss Gipsy Alexander. the last-named in the role of villain's accomplice, afford considerable satis- faction.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0232.txt | included=true | included =====
Domestic Pathos Next Week.

Geoffrey Fulton's fine new comedy-drama, "The Girl Who Broke Her Mother's Heart," has been secured for next week, and the author himself will appear amongst the first-rate com- pany who are to perform it. A visit will be well repaid.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
" ALL-STAH " COMPANY AT THE HIPPODROME.

Sara Williams provides one of the best turns in what has been not inaccurately styled an "all star" programme. She is the possessor of a brilliant soprano voice, over which she has great power of expression. Large 'audiences nightly have been held Spellbound by the magic of her singinrin that gem i from "Illaritana," "Scenes that are brightest." " The Four Boises give an acrobatic act that we do not remember to have seen beaten by any troupe that has appeared in the town. Their description, "the human aeroplanes," conveys some idea of the sensational nature of their per- formance, which, however, must be seen to be adequately appreciated. One of the performers is a funny man, with a good, old-fashioned red nose, which gives an extra ,spice to fare that needs no flavouring. , Novel indeed is the .contribution of Barnard and Bunny, and clever to-boot. This original couple give a realistic and therefore screamingly funny representation of the manceuvres and vocal hideousness of two amorous cats on the tiles at what respectable folk would consider an unseemly hour. Comedy juggling Of a very high order is per- formed by Zellini. Indeed, while he is on the boards the audience experiences hardly a moment that is not mirthful. " Thora " is a fraud, but so good a fraud that we forgive him. Ile enters to all appearances a lady, and the deception is intensified by the " conversation " that ensues between the per- former and the dummy. One is struck by what one thinks is a fine imitation of a male voice in the dummy's rendering of " Thora," and then when the ventriloquist has retired amidst enthusiastic applause, he returns and, suddenly snatching off a wig, stands revealed as a mere male.

As athletes. and particularly as hand balancers, the Lauri Brothers excel; and Lauri Howard, coon vocalist, complete a splendid bill.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====

What are truly described as great attractions will obtain next week. Pedersen Bros. appear as the famous comedy • aerial tricksters; Matthew Boulton and Co. present the spark- ling comedy "His Rest Day "; further comedy is contained in the "Father Time" duologue by Minnie Hunt and Rhys Thomas; Tom B. Barr will be very funny, as will also Royde and Villiers, the popular entertainers; Yazo is a violinist extraordinary; aosisted by Mdlle. Le Clere at the piano; the Four Bachelors will give vocal harmony most harmoniously-vocally; and the " Gaumont Graphic" is to supply the usual screen-interest.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
PAGEANT-DRAMA AT THE STAR

KINEMA

"Two Women "—a striking drama which has attracted much attention wherever it has been shown—is the film principal in the Star Kinema programmes to-day; and should not be missed by cinema habitues. The remaining dramas and comedies form a further feature.

During the first half of • this. week a good move was made by the Star Kinema manage- ment by securing for their chief feature "If I were King "—a film version of Justin Huntly McCarthy's famous historical problem-drama. Produced on a massive pageant scale and richly coloured, the film attracted large numbers of impressed picture-lovers. "Deliver The Goods" was a particularly appropriate war-story of how the enemy's underhand endeavours to mar a British munition-shop were frustrated. " Telling " war material was contained in "With The Allies In The East "—which described most graphically the manceuvres on the Gallipoli Peninsula. Particularly good were the scenes depicting the native fighters. Pathes' Animated Gazette" takes for its topical feature scenes at Queenstown amongst the stranded Arabic ,survivors. Further war- interest is supplied by a strong instalment of the Roll of Honour; whilst comic items are also shown.

POSTPONED ELECTIONS.

•. ..,

===== 0002601_19150904_art0238.txt | included=true | included =====
Town Clerk Summarises Provi.

sions Of New Act.

The Town Clerk has presented the following report to the General Purposes Committee on a recent Act of Parliament as it affects the Corporation:—

ELECTIONS AND REGISTRATION ACT, 1915.

This Act postpones for one year the statutory elections of Aldermen and Councillors and ex- tends the -term of office of existing Aldermen and Councillors for one year.. In the event of a casual- vacancy it is to be filled by 'means of the choice of the Council. Aldermen, as such, cannot vote at the election of an Alderman, but by this Act ' it would appear that all the Members of the Council may vote in any election 'index it. The Act provides that the Register of Voters shall 'remain In • force (unless, Parliament other- wise directs) until the 31st December, 1916. The Act annuls the appointment of Revising Barristers and provides that no Revision Courts shall be held in the present year. The cost of printing the Register of Voters is avoided, but the Act was passed too late to save the cost of printing the Overseers' Lists.

CHORISTER DEAD.

Mr. William Scotson's Half - Century Record.

A GREAT LOSS.

By the death of Mr. William Scotson—which, we regret to state, took place at his residence, 84, Manchester-road, early on Monday morning —Warrington choral circles sustain a loss as heavy as it was unexpected. A native of the town, Mr. Scotson was 65 years of age, and as a lad attended the National School, Church-street. He next joined the staff of the Warrington County Court, which posi- tion, however, he relinquished twelve years later, proceeding to Messrs. James Fairclough and Sons, Ltd., Mersey Flour Mills. With this staff he had remained since—through a period of some forty years. A zealous and honoured servant, he attainod the ppsition of cashier, and was held in high esteeni and respect by all members of the firm.

At business Mr. Scotson's last attendance was made so recently as Monday, August 2nd. It was during that night that he was seized with illness, and during the two ensuing weeks his condition gradually becathe more serious, even- tually compelling him to take to his bed—which he did not leave during the last fortnight. Towards the end his decline .was very rapid, and it was with unexpected suddenness that he Passed away.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PARISH CHURCH CHOIR.

" A true-hearted man, who is held high in the estimation of all who know him !" Such was the tribute of the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis) to Mr. Scotson ; and Particularly by the Parish Church the loss will be felt. That institution,

===== 0002601_19150904_art0241.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE MR. WILLIAM SCOTSON.

indeed, had become woven into his life—espe- cially the choral service. Its choir he joined as a boy, continuing in membership for no less than five years over the half-century. During that long period he came to be looked upon as an infallible attender, and the fine quality of his pure tenor voice was known as a feature of the services. Sc highly valued was be, indeed, that when in. October, 1910, he completed fifty years' service as a chorister, a special celebra- tion was held in the form of a dinner, at .Which he was the guest, and many fine tributes were paid to "the veteran," after he had been pre- sented by his Rector with a; beautiful inscribed silver rose bowl and vases.

In addition to his vocal services, Mr. Scotson had for a number of years 'acted as treasurer to the choir—a duty in which he took an especial pride. It is pleasing to note that his eldest son, Mr. John Scotson, has also been from boyhood a chorister, ably following his father's fine example. Between them father and son attained 81 years' service in the Parish Church choir.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MUSICAL SOCIETY.

No less will the loss be mourned by the War- rington Musical Society—of which Alr Scotson was one of the oldest members. Here again his chief interest was centred in choral work, and his last public appearance with the society was at their well-known interpretation of Mende's- sohn's "Elijah," given last winter in the Parish Church. But he also took a profound interest in all "good" music, as opposed to that of the lighter type so much in vogue of recent years. And an annual event in which he regularly assisted was the Warrington Training College concert.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0243.txt | included=true | included =====
OTHER INTERESTS.

Mr. Scotson was also an ex-member of the Warrington Cricket and Bowling Club, and some years ago bowled considerably. For a large number of years, too, he held the post of secre- tary to the National Sunday School Sick Club— he was the first to hold it. and only relinquished it a few months ago. In addition he was a Sunday school teacher.

Although a Conservative by political convic- tion, he took no active part in polities—although having frequently been invited to stand for the Council. This was only in accordance with his quiet and retiring disposition; all the good he did—and there is much of it—he did in an un- ostentatious manner.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0244.txt | included=true | included =====

LATE MRS. R. ROBERTS.

Well-known Wycliffe Lady's Sudden Death In London.

We announce with regret the death of Mrs. Roberts, widow of Mr. Richard Roberts—which occurred suddenly at her residence in Balham, London, on Sunday. Only the previous day Mrs. Roberts had taken a long walk; on Sunday she was seized with heart failure and passed away a few hours later. She was in her 86th year. Previous to her making her home in Balham, Mrs. Roberts was well known in Warrington. and many happy recollections are retained of her sweet nature and gentle disposition. At the Wycliffe Congregational Church particularly she was most deeply respected, having been a mem- ber practically all her life. At one time she enjoyed the somewhat rare distinction of having three .sons in the Town Council—Alderman Henry Roberts (who occupied the position of mayor). Councillor Fred It. Roberts and Coun- cillor WM. Roberts.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0247.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FUNERAL.

At the Warrington Cemetery on Thursday afternoon a considerable numbier attended the funeral. The Rev. John Yonge (the deceased lady's old pastor) conducted most impressively the service in the church, whilst the Rev. John Davies (son-in-law) officiated at the graveside. The mourners were: Councillor H. and Mrs. Roberts, Mr. and Mrs. W. Roberts (sons and daughters-in-law), Mr. Thomas Crampton (son- in-law), Rev. come, And answer the rousing call of the drum; As it calls o'er the road that is leading to peace —For this war from all war shall gain us release.

Jame, And havoc the world with their vileness and shame:

Last Night's Ambulance Class.

At the Police Station last night the new ambulance class made a modest but good start. Only those willing to volunteer for military ser- vice, Superintendent Baldry hat. stipulated, need apply for membership; and in response about a dozen volunteers attended. This num- ber, the Superintendent anticipates, will have made a good increase by next Friday's class is held.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0251.txt | included=true | included =====
Temperance Teaching In Schools.

Last night was Good Templars' night in con- nection with the Warrington Citizens' Temper- ance League. The meeting was held at the foot of Bewsey-street, and the speaker was Mr. Morris Jones, of Liverpool (secretary of the S.-W. Lanes. District). The Rev. W. T. Jaye presided. Mr. Jones said that he had read a letter front the Liverpool Corporation urging that temperance ideas be taught in ..he evening schools, and he thought that it would be a very good thing for the country. It had been con- sidered necessary in Liverpool to curtail the hours of the sale of drink, and as a result it was found that instead of ninety or a hundred cases appearing at the Police Court the number was not two-thirds of that.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0252.txt | included=true | included =====
Women And Thrift.

Next week the ladies are to have their say ,on the problem of economy. However eloquently men :may. talk about the need for economy the last word often lies with the women, for they are in charge of the treasury department in the home. The local committee, in calling a, conference of women at the Town Hall next Friday, has quoted some striking figures recently issued by the Board of Trade. These .figures show that the cost of food rose by one-third during the first twelve months of the war, while certain items in the food bill have leaped up much higher. The -cheaper cuts of meat have advanced 60 per cent., while granulated sugar shows an increase of 68 per cent. It is clear that in households where the income is fixed; the problem of economy has already been faced. One form of national service among women will be to work out in practice the mainten- ance of physical efficiency upon a reduced ex- penditure. This is exactly where experiences may profitably be shared, for in no department of life is it easier, to get into a groove than in household management. The Friday:conference is open to any ladies who care to attend. It is hoped that it may be followed by further steps to promote the wisest kinds of expenditure. -Miss Doulton-Edwards is announced as one of the speakers. She has been taking part in a similar movement in the city of Chester.

LANCE-CORPL. P. E. RICHARDSON.

Local Footballer Killed In The Dardanelles.

Lance-Corporal Peter Edward Richardson, whose parents reside at Knutsford, but who for some time prior to his enlistment, in August last year, was employed at the Stockton Heath Spade Works, has been killed in the Dardanelles by the explosion of a land-mine. Mr. and Mrs. Perrins, of 21, Chapel-street, Stockton Heath, with whom the deceased sol- dier 'resided, received the following letter from Lance-Corporal Wm. Richardson, a brother : "It is to my sorfow that I have to tell you the sad news that my brother has been killed. This was how it happened: We were aciyanc- ing and he stepped .on a land-mine, which blew him to pieces; so his death was so sud- den that he could not have felt any pa,in. I try to forget it, but it is hard. . . . &c." The deceased soldier was a member of the Stockton Heath Football Club. He enlisted in the Bth Battalion of the IsTorthumberland Fusiliers on August 19th, and was attached to "Y" Company. He had been at the Dar- danelles only eight days when he met his death. His age was twenty-five.

FLOODED PROPERTY.

Itcsident Coinplains Of An Insanitary

Nuisance.

Mr. G. Pilling presided at Tuesday evenirtg's meeting of the Parochial Committee. There were.also present: Messrs. J. Hallows. J. H. Clarke, W. Mclntyre, F. Pearson, A. Bottomley, F. Rose, J. McDermid, W. E. Hough (clerk to the District Council), S. Wright. (surveyor), and E. Bradshaw (clerk to the Parish Council). A long letter from Mr. Joseph Houghton, of 276, linufsford-road, Latchford, was read by the Clerk; once more calling attention to damage of his property at 17, Ellesmere-roa4as well as the household effects of tenants, by the flooding of the premises on August 10th, principally caused by the backing up of water from the gullies in front and behind the house, the main drain of which is altogether inadequate to take the vol- ume of Surface water that is required of it. The matter had been previously brought before the Council. When the gullies in London-road get choked up, all the water makes for Elles- mere-road, also from behind Egerton-street, Roman-road, and Brackley-street, and as the property in question is situated at the lowest part of the road, the result is that whenever there is a heavy downfall there is a backing up of the surface water on account of the drains not being able to receive it, and flooding of the property then takes place. "I can assure you," the letter read at an- other point, "that the house stank most ob- noxiously through he silt left in it, and was absolutely untenabß., and the tenants conse- quently had had to obtain sleeping accommoda- tion elsewhere. It is a scandalous shame, in face of the previous complaints, that there should be a repetition'of this thing, which has apparently been treated with little or no con- cern. Mr. Whitley can verify my statement, for he personally saw the state of affairs on the 10th inst., and I understand the Council's Sur- veyor, did, too. I should like to know what we pay rates for, surely not assessment, .because it is about time this sort of thing terminated. I hardly knoll- how to express my feelings on such a matter, after my previous complaints. Surely something better could have been done than has been done for the removal of this intolerable nuisance? . I shall look forward to the Council remedyinFAhe existing state of affairs; otherwise I shall resort to other measures, - as the nuisance has been endured long enough." Mr. Bottoniley said it was a very serious matter. The Property was .in an abominable condition. He'met Mr. Whitley the other day, and he said the stencb was :cruet The matter was important, not only to the 'council, but to the tenants and the landlord. There might be a serious outbreak of fever.

The Chairman said he thought the best way was to appoint a sub-committee to go into the question'.

The Council agreed with the suggestion, and the following were appointed the committee: Messrs. Pilling, Clarke, Pearson and Mclntyre, and the Surveyor.

RAILWAY FARE ALLOWED.

CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

===== 0002601_19150904_art0256.txt | included=true | included =====
Large Increase In Sales And

Membership.

The 219th quarterly meeting of the Warring- ton Ed. Co-operative Society, Limited, 114'6 held in the Co-operative. Hall, Cairo-street, on Monday. Mr. J., Upson .presided, and movo the adoption of the committee's report. The report showed that the sales of the Society for the half-year ended July 20th, had reached the total of £214,702, an increase of £40,425 over the corresponding period last year. During the three months 359 new members had joined, the • present net membership being 14,846. The President referred to the smallness of the attendance, and observed that it seemed a sign that the members were satisfied with the position of the Society, as it was more than probable that had there been a prospect of reduced divi- dend, the hall would have been crowded. no was, however.'glad to be able to report that in spite of the difficulties of trade in general, the Society had inure than maintained its position, and were able again to 'recommend a dividend of as. 24. in the L; to make the Usual grant to the education fund and the employes thrift fund, and- to carry a balance to the reserve fund, and also an additional £4OO to the dividend equalisation fund. He considered that the whole secret of the position lay in the fact Of their low rate of •

working expenses, which reflected very great credit on the officials who had tile management of the Society in hand. The adoption of the report was seconded ana carried unanimously._ The accounts were then taken one by one, and questions invited, but there was no comment, whatever. Mr. Green-Chappell made a brief statement on the position of the Society when his report came to be dealt with, and pointed out that the balance of assets over liabilities. including the profit balance amounted to £36,000, or equal to El 6s. for every El share in the Society.

WAR GRANTS AND INVESTMENTS.

The recommendations of the committee to approve a grant of £2O to the Warrington fund for providing a Red Cross motor, a grant of 5• weekly to the Bold-street Soldiers' Club, £5 to the French Relief Fund, and a further sugges- tion made at the meeting, of £lO for tbe Prisoners of War Fund for Warrington and dis- trict, were unanimously passed, as also was the approval of the investment by the Society of £2,000 in the Government 41,per cent. War Loan- The question of a subscription .to the Ancoats Hospital, Manchester, was raised by one of the members, and on the statement that a number of members received treatment there free of charge, it was decided to grant a subscription of £5 ss. per annum. The question of an allowance to one of the employes who bad joined the National Reserve was raised by a member, and the President promised a re-inquiry into the whole position.

COUNTY BOROUGH OF 'WARRINGTON. EDUCATION COMMITTEE. SECONDARY DAY SCHOOL AND TECHNICAL.. INSTITUTE. • The Committee invite applications for the position of Principal of the Secondary School and Technical Institute. The Secondary School is a Mixed School, including Bursars, Student Teachers and Pupil Teachers. Candidates must be not. less than 25 years of. age; and, be graduates of a British University. .Tne salary will be at the rate of £3OO, per annum, Increasing to £4OO by annual increments of £25. Canvassing members of the Committee either.. directly or indirectly will be considered a die.. qualification. Application must be made upon the official form, which can be obtained, together with further- particulars, from J. MOORE MURRAY, Secretary and Director.
